2097	Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 65 par. 1, 186, 192, 249, 306; Tax Code arts. 385, 388; Law 100 of 1993 art. 18; Decree 780 of 2016 art. 3.2.1.5 (Decree 1990 of 2016 art. 1); Ministry of Labour Circular 101 of 2025.
2098	4.4 Overtime
2099	Detail the statutory rules for overtime, including eligibility criteria and mandatory rates.
2100	The maximum ordinary working week. Labour Code art. 161, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 11: a maximum of 8 hours per day and 42 hours per week, distributable by agreement over five or six days, always preserving the rest day and without reducing salary. Law 2101 of 2021 art. 3 sets the gradual path; the final step to 42 hours takes effect on 15 July 2026.
2101	Period
2102	Maximum ordinary week
2103	15 July 2025 – 14 July 2026
2104	44 hours
2105	From 15 July 2026
2106	42 hours
2107	
2108	Where the week is distributed over five days so that the employee rests on Saturday and/or Sunday, the ordinary daily shift may be extended by up to two hours without paying supplementary work (Labour Code art. 164, confirmed by Circular 101 of 2025 §2.1).
2109	Eligibility. Overtime is due to every employee whose hours exceed the agreed ordinary day or, absent agreement, the statutory maximum. Exclusions and special regimes:
2110	Category
2111	Position
2112	Management, trust and confidential staff (empleados de dirección, confianza y manejo)
2113	Excluded from the maximum-hours rules. Law 2466 of 2025 art. 70 repealed Labour Code art. 162 literal b) — the exclusion for intermittent-activity workers and domestic staff is gone; only the direction/trust/management exclusion of literal a) survives
2114	Employees on all-inclusive salary
2115	Overtime is compensated in advance by the salary itself (Labour Code art. 132 num. 2) — except vacation, which is never absorbed
2116	Employees on 6-hour / 36-hour-week successive shift systems
2117	No night premium and no rest-day premium apply (Labour Code arts. 161(d) and 168 num. 1)
2118	Flexible daily shifts within 42 hours per week over up to 6 days, 4 to 9 continuous hours per day
2119	No supplementary-work premium (Labour Code art. 161(a))
2120	Minors 15–17
2121	6 hours per day, 30 hours per week, daytime only
2122	Minors over 17
2123	8 hours per day, 40 hours per week
2124	Piece-rate and agricultural workers
2125	Included — Circular 101 of 2025 §§2.3–2.4 is explicit that they are not excluded
2126	
2127	Volume limits. Labour Code art. 22 of Law 50 of 1990, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 13: "In no case may overtime hours, whether daytime or night-time, exceed two (2) hours per day and twelve (12) per week." Exceptions: private security (Law 1920 of 2018) and the health sector. Circular 101 derives the resulting maxima of ordinary plus overtime hours: 56 hours per week to 14 July 2026 and 54 hours per week from 15 July 2026.
2128	Ministry authorisation abolished; a register imposed instead. Law 2466 of 2025 art. 12 removed the requirement for Ministry of Labour authorisation to work overtime, and replaced it with an employer duty to keep a supplementary-work register recording, as a minimum: the activity performed, the total supplementary hours, the split between day and night hours, and evidence of payment. It must be available on demand to employees and to administrative and judicial authorities. Failure to keep it, or failure to pay supplementary work, allows inspectors to order the suspension of all supplementary work for six months.
2129	The night window moved. Labour Code art. 160, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 10: day work is 06:00 to 19:00 and night work is 19:00 to 06:00. Article 160 par. 2 delayed entry into force by six months from the 25 June 2025 sanction, so the 19:00 boundary has applied since 25 December 2025 and governs the whole of 2026. Two extra hours per day now attract the night premium compared with the pre-reform 21:00 boundary.
2130	Rates — Labour Code art. 168 (Law 50 of 1990 art. 24, not amended by the labour reform):
2131	Type
2132	Premium
2133	Note
2134	Night work, by reason of being night work
2135	+35% on the value of day work
2136	Excludes the 36-hour shift system
2137	Day overtime
2138	+25% on the ordinary day rate
2139	
2140	Night overtime
2141	+75% on the ordinary day rate
2142	
2143	
2144	Article 168 num. 4 is critical: "Each of the aforementioned premiums arises exclusively, that is to say, without being cumulated with any other." The night, day-overtime and night-overtime premiums are alternatives, not cumulative with one another. The obligatory-rest-day premium of art. 179 is a separate statutory head and is added to them.
2145	Work on an obligatory rest day or public holiday — Labour Code art. 179, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 14. The article sets the surcharge at 100%, with a transitional paragraph phasing it in:
2146	Period
2147	Rest-day / holiday premium
2148	1 July 2025 – 30 June 2026
2149	80%
2150	1 July 2026 – 30 June 2027
2151	90%
2152	From 1 July 2027
2153	100%
2154	
2155	An employer may voluntarily apply 100% from the outset.
2156	The three-value structure. Circular 101 of 2025 §4 is explicit that working on an obligatory rest day generates three independent payment obligations, not one:
2157	the paid rest day itself, at one day's ordinary salary (Labour Code art. 173) — first value;
2158	the hours actually worked on that day — second value;
2159	the additional premium on the hour value at 80% / 90% / 100% (art. 179) — third value.
2160	"Payment is made for the rest day, payment is made for the hours worked, and an additional premium is paid for working on Sundays or public holidays." An engine that pays only the hours plus the premium underpays by one day.
2161	Occasional versus habitual rest-day work. Article 179 par. 1: work on up to two obligatory rest days in a calendar month is occasional; three or more is habitual. Habitual rest-day work gives the employee the right to a compensatory rest day or to the corresponding remuneration, at the employee's election (Labour Code art. 180).
2162	Combined 2026 percentages (art. 168 premiums applied exclusively, plus the art. 179 premium where the day is an obligatory rest day):
2163	Hour type
2164	Jan–Jun 2026
2165	Jul–Dec 2026
2166	Ordinary day hour (06:00–19:00)
2167	100% (base)
2168	100% (base)
2169	Night premium (19:00–06:00)
2170	+35%
2171	+35%
2172	Day overtime
2173	+25%
2174	+25%
2175	Night overtime
2176	+75%
2177	+75%
2178	Rest-day / holiday premium, day hours
2179	+80%
2180	+90%
2181	Night premium on a rest day / holiday
2182	+35% +80% = +115%
2183	+35% +90% = +125%
2184	Day overtime on a rest day / holiday
2185	+25% +80% = +105%
2186	+25% +90% = +115%
2187	Night overtime on a rest day / holiday
2188	+75% +80% = +155%
2189	+75% +90% = +165%
2190	
2191	A defect in the official guidance, recorded for traceability. Circular 101 of 2025 §5.1 contains an internal inconsistency: after correctly stating the 80/90/100 phase-in in §4, it then states "supplementary work on Sundays and public holidays with a 75% premium (Labour Code art. 179)" — the pre-reform figure. The 80/90/100 schedule in §4, which tracks the statutory text of art. 179 as substituted by Law 2466 art. 14, governs. The 75% reference in §5.1 is a residual error and must not be configured.
2192	Explain how overtime is treated for income tax and social security calculations.
2193	Aspect
2194	Treatment
2195	Salary character
2196	Overtime and rest-day work are salary by express statutory definition — Labour Code art. 127 lists "the value of supplementary work or of overtime hours" and "the value of work on mandatory rest days"
2197	Social-security contribution base
2198	Fully included. No exemption, no cap other than the general 25-minimum-wage ceiling. Both employee (4% + 4% + solidarity fund) and employer (12% + 8.5% + occupational risk) contributions apply
2199	Payroll-levy base
2200	Fully included — Law 21 of 1982 art. 17 covers "the various constituent elements of salary"
2201	Statutory-benefit base
2202	Included in the service-bonus, severance-savings and interest bases. Excluded from the vacation base — Labour Code art. 192 num. 1 excludes only rest-day work and supplementary overtime hours from the vacation calculation. The night premium stays in the vacation base
2203	Withholding tax
2204	Fully taxable. Overtime enters the ordinary monthly taxable payments under both Procedure 1 and Procedure 2. There is no preferential or separate rate for overtime in Colombia, and no exempt overtime allowance
2205	Threshold effects
2206	A large overtime month can push the employee across the 10-minimum-wage payroll-levy exemption line, the 4-minimum-wage solidarity-fund trigger, or into a higher withholding band — all tested on that month's actual figures
2207	Payment timing
2208	With the salary of the period worked, or at the latest the following period (Labour Code art. 134 num. 2)
2209	Electronic payroll reporting
2210	Reported as discrete overtime and premium elements with a quantity, a start and end time, a percentage and an amount, under the annex's hour-type code table
2211	
2212	A reporting conflict, now resolved by tax-authority doctrine. The electronic payroll Technical Annex §5.5.5 (still at version 1.0, February 2021) hard-codes the pre-reform rest-day percentages — 100 / 75 / 150 / 110 for the four rest-day hour types — while the statute requires 80% in the first half of 2026 and 90% in the second. DIAN Concepto 013840 (int. 1568) of 1 October 2025 resolves the point: the annex's published percentage "must be read in accordance with the rules in force for each specific case", and the coexistence of different premium percentages within a single month "does not constitute any obstacle to the generation, transmission and subsequent validation" of the document. Employers must consolidate all earnings of the period including premiums settled at the statutorily applicable percentage. The engine therefore reports the real statutory percentage (80% or 90%), not the annex's legacy literal.
2213	Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 127, 134, 158, 159, 160, 161, 162, 164, 168, 169, 172, 173, 179, 180, 192 (as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 arts. 10, 11, 12, 13, 14 and 70); Law 50 of 1990 arts. 22 and 24; Law 2101 of 2021 arts. 3 and 4; Ministry of Labour Circular 101 of 22 September 2025 §§2.1, 2.3, 2.4, 3, 4, 5, 5.1; DIAN Concepto 013840 int. 1568 of 1 October 2025; Law 21 of 1982 art. 17.
2214	4.5 Bonuses
2215	Is there a statutory requirement to pay bonuses? If so, how are they calculated?
2216	Yes — the statutory service bonus (prima de servicios) is mandatory for every private-sector employee.
2217	Labour Code art. 306, as amended by Law 1788 of 2016 art. 1:
2218	"The employer is obliged to pay to its employee or employees the social benefit known as the service bonus (prima de servicios), which shall correspond to 30 days of salary per year, payable in two instalments, as follows: one half by 30 June at the latest and the other half no later than the first twenty days of December. It shall be recognised for the whole half-year worked or in proportion to the time worked. PARAGRAPH. This economic benefit includes domestic-service employees, family-service drivers, day workers and farm workers…"
2219	Element
2220	Rule
2221	Amount
2222	30 days' salary per year — 15 days per semester
2223	Instalments
2224	Half by 30 June, half by 20 December
2225	Proration
2226	For the whole semester worked or proportionally to the time worked
2227	Base
2228	Salary plus the transport allowance. Variable pay enters at the average of the semester
2229	Monthly accrual
2230	8.3333% (30 ÷ 360) of the base
2231	Legal character
2232	Not salary — Labour Code art. 307: "The annual bonus is not salary, nor shall it be counted as a salary factor in any case"
2233	Contribution base
2234	Excluded
2235	Payroll-levy base
2236	Excluded
2237	Coverage
2238	Universal, including domestic workers, family drivers, day workers and farm workers
2239	All-inclusive salary
2240	Absorbed — the all-inclusive salary compensates statutory bonuses in advance (Labour Code art. 132 num. 2)
2241	
2242	Set-off — Labour Code art. 308. Contractual or Christmas bonuses are set off against the statutory service bonus; where the statutory bonus is larger, the employer pays the difference. An employer already paying a 13th-month equivalent does not pay twice.
2243	Formula and worked example.
2244	Service bonus = (monthly salary + transport allowance) × days worked in the semester ÷ 360
2245	Employee on COP 2,400,000 plus the transport allowance of COP 249,095, having worked the full first semester of 2026 (180 days):
2246	(2,400,000 + 249,095) × 180 ÷ 360 = 2,649,095 × 0.5 = COP 1,324,547.50   payable by 30 June 2026
2247	Other statutory payments that are commonly but wrongly called bonuses. Severance savings, the interest on severance savings and vacation pay are separate statutory heads with their own rules, covered in Modules 8 and 9. There is no statutory Christmas bonus, no statutory profit-sharing and no statutory performance bonus in Colombia.
2248	Non-statutory bonuses — performance, sign-on, retention, referral, extra-statutory Christmas and vacation bonuses — are entirely contractual. They are salary if habitual, and therefore contributory, unless the parties have expressly agreed in writing that they are not salary under Labour Code art. 128, subject always to the 40% clamp.
2249	Is there a specific tax treatment for bonuses?
2250	Yes, and it differs sharply between the two withholding procedures. This is one of the most commonly mis-implemented rules in Colombian payroll.
2251	
2252	Withholding Procedure 1
2253	Withholding Procedure 2
2254	Statutory minimum service bonus
2255	Withheld separately, on its own read of the art. 383 table, not added to the month's salary. Tax Code art. 385: "In the case of the statutory minimum service bonus in the private sector … the 'amount to withhold' is the one shown against the bracket into which the respective bonus falls"
2256	Folded into the ordinary monthly base; the fixed percentage is applied to the combined figure. The 13 divisor in the semi-annual calculation already prorates the bonus effect
2257	Any excess above the statutory minimum bonus
2258	Added to the ordinary monthly base
2259	Ordinary monthly base
2260	Non-statutory bonuses
2261	Ordinary monthly base
2262	Ordinary monthly base
2263	Severance savings and interest
2264	Excluded from the monthly base under both procedures — they have their own art. 206 num. 4 treatment
2265	Same
2266	
2267	Everything else about a bonus's tax treatment is ordinary. Bonuses are taxable employment income, enter the depuration of Tax Code art. 388, and benefit from the 25% exempt-income deduction within the combined 40% and 1,340-tax-unit annual caps. There is no preferential bonus rate, no separate annualisation method and no tax-free bonus allowance in Colombia.

2304	Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 128, 132, 306, 307, 308 (art. 306 as amended by Law 1788 of 2016 art. 1); Tax Code arts. 206, 383, 385, 386, 388; Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30.
2305	4.6 Wage Classification (Country-Specific)
2306	Are there country-specific classifications for wages critical for social security or tax calculations?
2307	Yes — four, and they are the defining feature of Colombian payroll. Getting any one of them wrong mis-states either the contribution base, the levy base, the benefit base or the withholding base.
2308	#
2309	Classification
2310	Statutory source
2311	What it decides
2312	1
2313	Salary vs non-salary (salario vs no constitutivo de salario)
2314	Labour Code arts. 127 and 128
2315	Whether a payment enters the contribution base, the payroll-levy base and the statutory-benefit base
2316	2
2317	The 40% non-salary clamp
2318	Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30
2319	How much validly non-salary pay may nonetheless be excluded from the contribution base
2320	3
2321	Ordinary salary vs all-inclusive salary (salario integral)
2322	Labour Code art. 132 num. 2–3; Law 100 art. 18
2323	Whether the contribution base and the payroll-levy base are 100% or 70% of the salary, and which statutory benefits are absorbed
2324	4
2325	Statutory benefits (prestaciones sociales) vs salary vs paid rest
2326	Labour Code Titles VIII–IX; art. 128
2327	Which payments are excluded from the contribution base, and which — like vacation — are not
2328	
2329	Provide a detailed definition for each classification.
2330	1. Salary vs non-salary.
2331	Labour Code art. 127 (Law 50 of 1990 art. 14) defines salary as "everything the employee receives in money or in kind as direct consideration for the service, whatever the form or designation adopted, such as bonuses, salary supplements, habitual gratuities, the value of supplementary work or of overtime hours, the value of work on mandatory rest days, sales percentages and commissions."
2332	Article 128 (Law 50 of 1990 art. 15) excludes: occasional and purely gratuitous sums; profit participation and solidarity-economy surpluses; amounts given "not for the employee's benefit, nor to enrich their estate, but in order to perform their duties properly" — representation expenses, means of transport, work tools; the statutory benefits of Titles VIII and IX; and "habitual or occasional benefits or allowances agreed collectively or contractually … where the parties have expressly stipulated that they do not constitute salary", such as food, housing, clothing and extra-statutory vacation, service or Christmas bonuses.
2333	Three tests for a valid non-salary pact: it must be (a) express, (b) written, and (c) attach to a benefit or allowance, not to consideration for the service itself. A "non-salary" label on what is plainly remuneration for work will not survive a contribution audit.
2334	2. The 40% non-salary clamp.
2335	Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30, verbatim:
2336	"Without prejudice to what is provided for other purposes, for the purposes related to articles 18 and 204 of Law 100 of 1993, the labour payments of private-sector employees that do not constitute salary may not exceed 40% of total remuneration."
2337	A valid non-salary pact removes a payment from the labour-law salary base permanently — it never enters the statutory-benefit base or the overtime base. But it removes the payment from the social-security contribution base only up to 40% of total remuneration. Everything above that line is added back for health, pension and occupational risk while remaining non-salary for the benefits. Two divergent bases, permanently. The clamp bites on articles 18 and 204 of Law 100 only — it does not reach the payroll levies, which have their own base.
2338	3. Ordinary salary vs all-inclusive salary.
2339	Labour Code art. 132 num. 2 (Law 50 of 1990 art. 18): where the employee earns an ordinary salary above 10 minimum wages, the parties may agree in writing a salary that additionally compensates in advance the statutory benefits, premiums and allowances — night work, overtime, rest-day and holiday work, statutory and extra-statutory bonuses, severance savings and their interest, subsidies and in-kind supplies — except vacation.
2340	Element
2341	Rule
2342	2026
2343	Minimum
2344	10 minimum wages + a benefit factor of at least 30%
2345	13 minimum wages = COP 22,761,765
2346	Contribution base
2347	70% of the all-inclusive salary (Law 100 art. 18 final paragraph; Decree 1072 art. 2.2.4.3.3 for occupational risk)
2348	At the floor: 15,933,235.50, rounded up
2349	Payroll-levy base
2350	Also 70% — Labour Code art. 132 num. 3: "the contributions shall be reduced by thirty percent (30%)"
2351	
2352	Vacation
2353	Still owed in full, 15 working days per year, on the whole all-inclusive salary
2354	
2355	Tax
2356	The former exemption of the benefit factor was replaced by the 25% exempt-income deduction (Tax Code art. 206 par. 2)
2357	
2358	
2359	4. Statutory benefits vs salary vs paid rest — and why vacation is the trap.
2360	Statutory benefits (prestaciones sociales) are employer-borne entitlements under Titles VIII and IX: the service bonus, severance savings, the interest on them, work clothing and sickness assistance. They are excluded from salary by art. 128 and therefore from the contribution base.
2361	Vacation is not a statutory benefit. It is paid rest. The consequences are counter-intuitive and must be configured deliberately:
2362	Vacation days carry contributions on the last IBC reported before the vacation began, normally the prior month's IBC, not the current one (Decreto 780 de 2016 art. 3.2.5.1). The employee is still paid at the current ordinary rate, and health and pension contributions are due in full on that earlier base. For occupational risk (ARL), vacation is a reportable change event (novedad), and no employer ARL contribution is due for the vacation days (Decreto 1072 de 2015 art. 2.2.4.2.1.6 num. 1.3, from Decreto 1772 de 1994 art. 19 as amended by Decreto 1528 de 2015). On PILA, the ARL IBC may differ from the health/pension IBC when the vacation change event is reported (Anexo Técnico 2, field 44). Función Pública Concepto 091961 de 2022, on public servants, takes the opposite view without addressing that decree article; the decree governs.
2363	Vacation pay enters the payroll-levy base, because Law 21 of 1982 art. 17 expressly covers
2364	"descansos remunerados".
2365	Its own calculation base excludes overtime and rest-day work only (Labour Code art. 192 num. 1) — the
2366	night premium and commissions stay in.
2367	An all-inclusive salary does not absorb it (art. 132 num. 2 expressly excepts it), so vacation is owed
2368	on the full all-inclusive salary while contributions run on 70%.
2369	Create a reference table classifying common pay components.

2619	Module 5: Social Security & Pension Systems
2620	Purpose: To detail the rules for all mandatory social security and pension schemes.
2621	Wage-caliber declaration (control B2). Every social-security floor, ceiling and trigger in Colombia derives from one single published figure — the statutory minimum monthly wage (SMMLV) — and from no average-wage series of any kind. Colombia's statistics office publishes average and median wage series, and the minimum-wage decree itself cites a living-wage study, consumer-price inflation, total-factor productivity, labour productivity per hour and per employed person, and gross-domestic-product growth — but none of these enters any payroll parameter. They are inputs to the annual political negotiation that fixes the minimum wage, not calibers in their own right. There is therefore no possibility of mixing calibers in Colombian payroll, and the reconciliation is trivial and exact:

2646	
2647	The only other calibration unit in Colombian payroll is the tax unit (UVT), COP 52,374 for 2026, and it indexes tax parameters exclusively — never a social-security parameter. The two units are never mixed. The statutory transport allowance of COP 249,095 is a fixed peso amount, not a caliber, and indexes nothing.

2715	Total standard employee burden: 8.0%, rising to 10.0% at the top of the solidarity-fund scale. Total standard employer burden: 12% + 8.5% + occupational risk + 9% levies, before the exemption.
2716	Three of the eight are exonerated for lower-paid employees. Tax Code art. 114-1 exonerates employer health 8.5%, the apprenticeship training levy 2% and the family welfare levy 3% for employees earning less than 10 minimum wages, where the employer is a legal person that is an income-tax filer or a natural person with two or more employees. Pension, occupational risk and the family compensation fund 4% are never exonerated. See Module 5.3.
2717	Newest-law check — the 2024 pension reform is NOT the operative law for FY2026 (control C2). Colombia enacted a complete replacement of its pension architecture, Law 2381 of 2024 ("establishing the Integral Social Protection System for Old Age, Invalidity and Death from Common Causes"), which replaces the two competing regimes of Law 100 of 1993 with a pillar system. It is the newest pension law on the statute book and it is the reason this guide states its pension basis explicitly. It does not govern any 2026 payroll period, and the rates in the table above are the Law 100 of 1993 rates as amended by Law 797 of 2003. The judicial history that produces that result:
2718	Step
2719	Instrument
2720	Effect
2721	Enacted
2722	Law 2381 of 2024, art. 94
2723	Set its own entry into force at 1 July 2025
2724	Suspended
2725	Constitutional Court Auto 841 of 18 June 2025
2726	Suspended that entry into force, sparing only art. 12 and art. 76, and ordered the Chamber of Representatives to re-run the fourth plenary debate within 30 working days (minimum three hours) to cure a procedural defect
2727	Upheld, deferred
2728	Constitutional Court Judgment C-264 of 25 August 2026 (case D-15989)
2729	Held art. 94 constitutional on the understanding that the upheld provisions take effect on 1 April 2027. Over 80 articles were upheld against the procedural challenge. Nine were returned to the Chamber for a cured debate: art. 11 inciso 4, art. 19 literal k, art. 14, art. 23 transitory paragraph, art. 36, art. 63 inciso 2, art. 84 num. 5, art. 92 inciso 1 and art. 93. The Court reviewed procedure only, not substance
2730	
2731	Consequence for configuration. For every pay period from 1 January to 31 December 2026 the engine must apply Law 100 of 1993 as amended by Law 797 of 2003 — the rates, bands, floor and ceiling set out in this module. No part of Law 2381 of 2024 may be configured as live for 2026. The reform is a 2027 change-control item, and because judgment C-264 of 2026 was handed down on 25 August 2026 the parameters below must be re-verified before the April 2027 cut-over.

2758	n/a

2760	
2761	The employer's collection duty is unchanged in substance: the employer pays its own share and withholds the employee's share at the moment of payment (Law 2381 of 2024 art. 21, matching Law 100 of 1993 art. 22).
2762	Are contributions managed at a national or sub-national level?
2763	National. There is no departmental or municipal social-security contribution, no regional rate variation and no sub-national scheme. All rates, bases, floors and ceilings above apply uniformly across the country.
2764	Three points of institutional, not rate, variation:
2765	Point
2766	Nature
2767	Occupational-risk class
2768	Varies by work centre, not by geography. A company with two work centres legitimately carries two risk classes and two rates. Where a person performs several occupations, they contribute at the highest risk class (Decree 1072 of 2015 art. 2.2.4.2.5.6)
2769	Family compensation fund
2770	Organised by department; the receiving institution code differs, the 4% rate does not
2771	Small-business levy scales
2772	Law 1429 of 2010 provides a graduated scale for qualifying micro and small enterprises, with a special regime for Amazonas, Guainía and Vaupés. Visible in the contribution-return rate tables as contributor classes C and D. Not applicable to standard corporate payroll
2773	
2774	Collection is national but intermediated. The employer never transacts directly with the government for social security. Contributions are self-assessed and paid through a licensed private information operator (operador de información) which files the integrated contribution return and distributes the money to the health insurer, pension administrator, occupational-risk insurer, family compensation fund, apprenticeship service and family welfare institute. The audit counterparty is the UGPP, which cross-matches tax-authority data against filed contribution returns.
2775	5.1.1 Contribution Basis Definition
2776	For each mandatory contribution, provide the statutory definition of the earnings base.
2777	Health, pension, solidarity fund and occupational risk — the contribution base (Ingreso Base de Cotización, IBC).
2778	Law 100 of 1993 art. 18, as substituted by Law 797 of 2003 art. 5, verbatim highlights:
2779	"The limit of the contribution base shall be twenty-five (25) legal monthly minimum wages in force, for public- and private-sector employees." "Contributions for employees whose remuneration is agreed under the integral-salary arrangement shall be calculated on 70% of that salary." "In no case may the contribution base be lower than one legal monthly minimum wage in force." Par. 1: where the employee has two or more employers, contributions are made proportionally to each remuneration and accumulate up to the legal ceiling; "contributions to the health system must be made on the same base".
2780	For an integral-salary employee on vacation, the 70% rule and the prior-IBC vacation rule combine: the IBC for the vacation days is the last IBC reported before the vacation began, which for this employee is already 70% of the integral salary, normally the PRIOR month's. It is not 70% of the vacation payment and not 70% of the current month's salary. The 70% rule is Ley 100 de 1993 art. 18 (as amended by Ley 797 de 2003 art. 5) and Decreto 780 de 2016 art. 2.2.1.1.2.1, restated in Ley 2381 de 2024 art. 22. The prior-IBC rule is Decreto 780 de 2016 art. 3.2.5.1 (see Module 5.2 for the ordinary-salary worked example).
2781	Health uses the same base as pension — Law 100 art. 204 par. 1 — and occupational risk likewise (Decree 1072 of 2015 art. 2.2.4.3.3). The content of the base is the set of payments that are salary under Labour Code arts. 127 and 128, clamped by Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30.
2782	Family compensation fund, apprenticeship training levy and family welfare levy — the payroll-levy base (nómina mensual de salarios).
2783	Law 21 of 1982 art. 17, verbatim:
2784	"… the monthly salary payroll means the totality of the payments made in respect of the various constituent elements of salary within the meaning of the labour law, whatever their designation, and in addition those made for statutory and collectively agreed or contractual paid rest periods." Foreign-currency payments are included, converted at the official rate on the last day of the month.
2785	Clarify the legal definitions of any specific terms used.
2786	Term
2787	Definition
2788	Contribution base (IBC)
2789	The monthly sum of salary-constituting payments, after the 40% clamp, the 70% all-inclusive rule, the 1-minimum-wage floor and the 25-minimum-wage ceiling, rounded up to the whole peso
2790	Payroll-levy base
2791	The total of all salary elements plus statutory and contractual paid rest days, with no ceiling
2792	Salary (salario)
2793	Labour Code art. 127 — everything received in money or in kind as direct consideration for the service, whatever its name
2794	Non-salary (no constitutivo de salario)
2795	Labour Code art. 128 — occasional gratuities, profit participation, sums given to enable the work, the statutory benefits, and expressly-pacted benefits and allowances
2796	All-inclusive salary (salario integral)
2797	Labour Code art. 132 num. 2 — a written salary above 13 minimum wages compensating benefits, premiums and allowances in advance, except vacation
2798	Statutory benefits (prestaciones sociales)
2799	Titles VIII–IX of the Labour Code — the service bonus, severance savings, interest on severance savings, work clothing, sickness assistance
2800	Solidarity account (subcuenta de solidaridad)
2801	The solidarity pension fund sub-account subsidising contributions of low-income and informal workers
2802	Subsistence account (subcuenta de subsistencia)
2803	The solidarity pension fund sub-account funding the non-contributory old-age benefit
2804	Public defined-benefit regime (Régimen de Prima Media, RPM)
2805	The pay-as-you-go pension regime administered by Colpensiones
2806	Private individual-account regime (Régimen de Ahorro Individual con Solidaridad, RAIS)
2807	The funded regime administered by private pension administrators
2808	Occupational risk class
2809	I to V, assigned to the work centre by economic activity under Decree 768 of 2022
2810	Exemption (exoneración)
2811	The Tax Code art. 114-1 relief from employer health, apprenticeship-training and family-welfare contributions for employees below 10 minimum wages
2812	Change event (novedad)
2813	A within-month event reported on the contribution return that alters the base or the contributory days
2814	
2815	Deliverable: Comprehensive summary of current contribution rates, across regions, residential statuses, tiers, ceilings, floors, and variations by age or gender

2961	No occupational-risk contribution during the novelty. For the days of an incapacity, of vacation taken, of unpaid leave or suspension, and after termination, no occupational-risk contribution accrues to the employer (Decree 1072 of 2015 art. 2.2.4.2.1.6, novelties 1.2, 1.3, 1.4 and 1.6, from Decree 1772 of 1994 art. 19 as modified by Decree 1528 of 2015). Health and pension contributions do continue during incapacity (on the value of the incapacity) and vacation (on the last contribution base reported before it began) (Decree 780 of 2016 arts. 3.2.1.10 and 3.2.5.1).

2999	Employed old-age pensioner
3000	No pension contribution; health, occupational risk and the levies continue. Reported with worker sub-type 01. Where the employer pays a pension itself, the pensioner's own health contribution on each monthly pension (mesada) is 4% for a pension of 1 minimum wage, 10% above 1 and up to 3 minimum wages, and 12% above 3 minimum wages, from 2024 (Law 100 of 1993 art. 204 par. 5, inciso added by Law 2294 of 2023 art. 78; Resolution MSPS 1271 of 2023 art. 1, PILA Technical Annex 1)
3001	Employee at or near retirement age
3002	No rate variation. Retirement age (57 women / 62 men under the public defined-benefit regime) affects entitlement, not the payroll rate
3003	High-risk activity employees (Decree-Law 2090 of 2003)
3004	An additional employer pension contribution of 10 percentage points (26% total) and a reduced retirement age — see the note below
3005	
3006	Variation by gender: none. There is no gender-based rate, threshold or ceiling in any of the eight contributions. Labour Code art. 143, as amended by Law 1496 of 2011, expressly forbids salary differences by gender, and the social-security statutes are gender-neutral throughout. Retirement age differs (57 for women, 62 for men in the public defined-benefit regime), which affects entitlement and the point at which the pension contribution ceases, but never the contribution rate.
3007	High-risk pension activities. Decree-Law 2090 of 2003 establishes the high-risk activities that carry an additional employer pension contribution and a reduced retirement age, and the electronic payroll XML carries a required AltoRiesgoPension flag. The contribution-return rate table of Resolution 2388 of 2016 shows a total pension rate of 26% for a high-risk contributor (against the standard 16%), implying an additional 10 percentage points borne by the employer. The additional employer contribution is stated in the primary source. Decreto-Ley 2090 de 2003 art. 5: 'El monto de la cotización especial para las actividades de alto riesgo es el previsto en la Ley 100 de 1993, más diez (10) puntos adicionales a cargo del empleador'. So 26% total pension (16% standard + 10 points, employer-borne) is the 2026 rate. Regime validity: art. 8, as extended by Decreto 2655 de 2014, limited the special regime to workers in those activities up to 31 December 2024; workers already affiliated stay covered (art. 8 inc. 3). MinTrabajo Resolución 6072 de 2024 gave operating instructions. Decreto 1435 of 24 December 2025 (D.O. 26-12-2025) extended the regime 'hasta cuando el Congreso de la República expida la ley correspondiente', and it covers workers in those activities 'con independencia de la fecha de su vinculación laboral'. Still open: how the 10 points apply to workers who started a high-risk activity between 1 January 2025 and the decree's publication (no rule found). Configure the 10 points; re-check if Congress legislates (the decree requires a bill within 12 months).
3008	5.2 Calculation Logic and Edge Cases
3009	What is the step-by-step calculation logic for all types of social security contributions?
3010	STEP 1  Build the CONTRIBUTION BASE        1.1  Sum salary-constituting payments of the month (Labour Code arts. 127/128)             - transport and connectivity allowances EXCLUDED             - service bonus, severance savings and interest EXCLUDED             - vacation taken: health and pension on the IBC reported before the vacation began, not on the vacation pay (Decree 780 of 2016 art. 3.2.5.1); no occupational-risk contribution for those days (Decree 1072 of 2015 art. 2.2.4.2.1.6 num. 1.3)             - vacation paid in cash (during employment or on termination) EXCLUDED (CE S4 exp. 26941 of 2024; SU exp. 25185 of 2021, rule 1)        1.2  Apply the 40% clamp: if PACTED non-salary payments > 40% of total remuneration,             add the excess back into the base   (Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30)        1.3  All-inclusive salary: base = 70% of the salary        1.4  Floor: base >= 1 minimum wage, pro-rated to contributory days        1.5  Ceiling: base <= 25 minimum wages = 43,772,625        1.6  Round the base UP to the nearest whole pesoSTEP 2  Build the PAYROLL-LEVY BASE (separate, no ceiling)        2.1  Sum all salary elements PLUS statutory and contractual paid rest days (vacation taken, paid in cash or paid on termination)        2.2  All-inclusive salary: base = 70%        2.3  No floor, no ceiling        2.4  Round UP to the nearest whole pesoSTEP 3  EMPLOYEE deductions on the contribution base        Health         4%        Pension        4%    (0% for an employed old-age pensioner)        Solidarity fund 1% if base >= 4 minimum wages        Subsistence    0.2%-1.0% if base >= 16 minimum wages, by band        Round EACH amount UP to the nearest multiple of 100STEP 4  Evaluate the EXEMPTION (Tax Code art. 114-1)        exempt := employer_is_income_tax_filing_legal_person                  AND month_earnings < 10 x minimum wage      # STRICTLY less than        (a natural-person employer qualifies only if it employs 2 or more workers)STEP 5  EMPLOYER contributions        Pension                     12%   of contribution base            always        Health                      8.5%  of contribution base            0 if exempt        Occupational risk           class rate of contribution base       always        Family compensation fund    4%    of payroll-levy base            always        Apprenticeship training     2%    of payroll-levy base            0 if exempt        Family welfare              3%    of payroll-levy base            0 if exempt        Round EACH amount UP to the nearest multiple of 100STEP 6  Report contributory DAYS per subsystem and any change events
3011	Scope of the 40% clamp. The 40% limit of Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30 applies only to payments that would remunerate the service (salary in nature, Labour Code art. 127) but were agreed as excluded from the contribution base under Labour Code art. 128 and Law 344 of 1996 art. 17 — the WTC wage types marked 'Non-Salary - pacted'. Payments that are non-salary by their nature, because they do not remunerate the service — the transport allowance, occasional per diem, means and tools to do the job, work clothing and footwear (dotación), payments by mere liberality — never enter the contribution base, whatever their amount, and are not subject to the clamp (WTC 'Non-Salary - non-remunerative'). Consejo de Estado, Sección Cuarta, sentencia de unificación rad. 05001-23-33-000-2016-02496-01 (25185), 9 December 2021, rule 3: "El pacto de 'desalarización' no puede exceder el límite previsto en el artículo 30 de la Ley 1393 de 2010, es decir, el 40% del total de la remuneración." The ruling does not define the 'total de la remuneración' against which the 40% is measured; UGPP's guidance for employers (ABC Empleadores, 2025) defines it as all salary payments plus the pacted non-salary payments plus paid leave, leaving out indemnities, unpaid leave, incapacities and social benefits. Payments that are non-salary by nature are therefore outside both the numerator and the total. Vacation pay is not part of the total: it is not in the 2025 formula, and UGPP's 2021 guidance excluded it expressly. Where the nature of a payment is unclear, it is treated as pacted and the Non-Salary Pact Flag in the DD decides.
3012	Are contributions based on actual or standard salary?
3013	Actual salary, with four statutory adjustments — the floor, the ceiling, the 70% all-inclusive rule and the 40% clamp. Colombia has no notional, banded or standard contribution salary; there is no class system and no scheduled contribution wage.
3014	Two consequences worth stating explicitly:
3015	A variable-pay employee has a different contribution base every month, and can legitimately cross
3016	the 4-minimum-wage solidarity trigger, the 10-minimum-wage exemption line and the 25-minimum-wage ceiling in different directions in successive months.
3017	Where actual salary falls below the floor — a partial month, a low-paid part-time employee — the base is
3018	raised to 1 minimum wage, pro-rated to the contributory days where a valid change event reduces them. The employee still contributes 4% + 4% of that raised base even though the salary was lower.
3019	A special case: the apprentice. Decree 0223 of 5 March 2026 art. 2.2.6.3.3.4 par. 2 provides that "in any event the contribution base must be [at] one (1) legal monthly minimum wage in force" and that the employee-side deductions are computed on that base. So an apprentice in the classroom phase, receiving 75% of the minimum wage, nonetheless contributes on a full minimum wage.
3020	Describe rules for eligibility, joining month, termination month, mid-period hires, mid-period terminations, and mid-period salary changes.
3021	Eligibility. Affiliation to health, pension and occupational risk is compulsory from the first day of the employment relationship, with no waiting period, no minimum hours and no minimum earnings. Two timing rules matter:
3022	Occupational-risk coverage starts the calendar day after affiliation (Decree 1072 of 2015
3023	art. 2.2.4.2.5.4), and a new hire must be reported to the risk insurer no later than the business day following the start date (art. 2.2.4.3.6). An employee who starts work before affiliation is uninsured, and the employer bears the occupational-risk benefit directly.
3024	Health and pension affiliation must be effective from the start date.
3025	Joining month. Contributions are due for the days actually worked, with a hire change event carrying the contributory day count. The base is the actual earnings for those days, floored at the pro-rated minimum wage. The full monthly rates apply — there is no reduced first-month rate.
3026	Termination month. Contributions are due to the termination date, with a termination change event. Termination settlement components are treated as follows:
3027	Component
3028	In the contribution base?
3029	Salary to the termination date
3030	Yes
3031	Accrued vacation paid in cash
3032	No for health, pension and occupational risk – vacation paid in cash is not salary (Consejo de Estado, Sección Cuarta, exp. 26941 of 4 April 2024; unification ruling exp. 25185 of 9 December 2021, rule 1); it does enter the payroll-levy base (Law 21 of 1982 art. 17)
3033	Accrued service bonus
3034	No
3035	Accrued severance savings and interest
3036	No
3037	Dismissal indemnity
3038	No
3039	Payment in lieu of notice
3040	No
3041	
3042	Mid-period hire and termination in the same month. Both change events are reported; the base is the earnings of the days actually worked.
3043	Mid-period salary change. Split the month at the effective date, prorate each rate over its own days, sum to the monthly base, and report a salary variation change event. Where an increase takes the employee across the 10-minimum-wage line for that month's total earnings, the exemption is lost for the whole month — the test is on the month, not on the segment.
3044	Within-month change events the engine must support (contribution-return mnemonics, verified from Resolution 2388 of 2016 Technical Annex 2):
3045	Event
3046	Meaning
3047	Effect on contributions
3048	ING

3062	Base recomputed
3063	SLN
3064	Suspension or unpaid leave
3065	Health rate moves to 8.5% or 0% and pension to 12% or 16% depending on the variant; the payroll levies fall to 0%
3066	IGE
3067	General-illness incapacity
3068	Health and pension continue, with the value of the incapacity as the IBC (Decree 780 of 2016 arts. 3.2.1.10 and 3.2.5.1); no occupational-risk contribution during the incapacity (Decree 1072 of 2015 art. 2.2.4.2.1.6 num. 1.2)
3069	LMA
3070	Maternity or paternity leave
3071	Contributions continue on the base
3072	VAC / LR
3073	Vacation or paid leave
3074	Vacation: health and pension continue on the IBC of the month before the vacation (Decree 780 of 2016 art. 3.2.5.1); no occupational-risk contribution during the vacation (Decree 1072 of 2015 art. 2.2.4.2.1.6 num. 1.3); the vacation pay is in the CCF / SENA / ICBF base (Law 21 of 1982 art. 17; Law 89 of 1988 art. 1 par. 1). Paid leave: contributions continue on the base
3075	IRL
3076	Occupational accident or disease incapacity
3077	Contributions continue
3078	AVP
3079	Voluntary pension contribution
3080	Additional voluntary amount
3081	VCT
3082	Change of work centre
3083	Occupational-risk class may change
3084	
3085	Do different payment frequencies affect contribution rates or parameters?
3086	No. Rates, floors, ceilings and triggers are all monthly parameters, independent of how often the employee is paid. A weekly-paid employee and a monthly-paid employee on the same annual salary contribute identically.
3087	Aspect
3088	Effect of frequency
3089	Rates
3090	None
3091	Floor and ceiling
3092	None — both are monthly
3093	Solidarity-fund trigger
3094	None — tested on the monthly base
3095	Exemption test
3096	None — tested on the monthly earnings
3097	Filing frequency
3098	None — the contribution return is monthly regardless
3099	Contributory days
3100	Reported per subsystem for the month, whatever the number of payments
3101	
3102	The only frequency-sensitive interaction is the five-payment weekly month discussed in Module 1.3 — which changes the amount of the monthly base, not any parameter.
3103	Detail variations based on employee age or gender.
3104	Age.
3105	Age or status
3106	Variation
3107	15–17, lawfully employed
3108	No rate variation. Restricted hours (6 per day, 30 per week, daytime only) reduce the base
3109	18 and above
3110	Standard rates
3111	Employed old-age pensioner
3112	Pension contribution ceases entirely — neither the 4% employee nor the 12% employer share is due. Health, occupational risk and the levies continue. Worker sub-type 01
3113	Employee in a high-risk activity
3114	Additional employer pension contribution and reduced retirement age (Decree-Law 2090 of 2003) — see Module 5.1.1
3115	Retirement age (57 women / 62 men, public regime)
3116	Affects entitlement, not the rate. Reaching the age does not by itself stop the contribution; being a pensioner in active service does
3117	
3118	Gender: no variation of any kind. No gender-differentiated rate, base, floor, ceiling or trigger exists in any of the eight contributions.
3119	Is there a minimum number of working days required for contributions?
3120	No. There is no minimum-days, minimum-hours or minimum-earnings qualifying condition. One day of employment generates a contribution obligation for that day, with the base floored at the pro-rated minimum wage.
3121	Three refinements:
3122	The floor is pro-rated to contributory days, not applied in full to a part-month. An employee hired
3123	on the 20th contributes on 11 days at the pro-rated floor, not on a full minimum wage.
3124	Unpaid leave (change event SLN) reduces the contributory days. The contribution-return rate table
3125	shows that in this case health moves to 8.5% or 0% and pension to 12% or 16% depending on the variant, and the payroll levies fall to 0%.
3126	Part-time contributions. Law 2466 of 2025 art. 34 permits micro-enterprises and households to
3127	pay social security on a part-time basis, computed in weeks and settled at each month end, and permits proportional contributions where monthly income is below the minimum wage. Access requires commercial registration, accounting books and an internal register of employment contracts. Explicitly outside standard corporate payroll scope, and monitored by the UGPP; the part-time contribution may not be used where the work is in fact full-time (art. 34 par. 2).
3128	How are contributions calculated for Board of Directors who are also employees?
3129	Colombia distinguishes sharply between a board mandate and an employment relationship, and the payroll treatment follows the substance of the relationship, not the title.
3130	Situation
3131	Employment relationship?
3132	Social security
3133	Withholding tax
3134	Director who is also an employee — an executive director holding an employment contract
3135	Yes
3136	Full employee treatment on the whole remuneration, salary and director's fees alike, where the fees are consideration for the service. Employer 12% + 8.5% + occupational risk + levies; employee 4% + 4% + solidarity fund
3137	Art. 383 progressive table
3138	Non-executive director receiving only attendance fees (honorarios de junta directiva)
3139	No — this is a corporate mandate, not employment
3140	Not an employee contribution. The director contributes as an independent worker on a base of 40% of the monthly fee, with a 1-minimum-wage floor (operating practice; legal basis to be confirmed)
3141	Art. 383 table applies — Tax Code art. 383 par. 2 extends the employment table to non-employment work income using "pagos mensualizados": total contract value less mandatory health and pension contributions, divided by the months of the contract
3142	Statutory legal representative on an employment contract
3143	Yes
3144	Full employee treatment; generally management staff excluded from the maximum-hours rules
3145	Art. 383 table
3146	
3147	Two engine consequences.
3148	Where a person is both an employee and a fee-earning director of the same or a related company, the
3149	two income streams have different social-security bases — full salary for the employment, 40% of fees for the mandate — but the same withholding table. They accumulate against the shared 25-minimum-wage ceiling.
3150	Directors' fees paid to a non-employee are outside payroll scope for social security and are handled
3151	as an independent-contractor payment. Mercans scope covers the employment relationship only; the fee-only director is descoped and the client retains responsibility.
3152	Are year-end adjustments required?
3153	No year-end reconciliation of social security is required. Colombian contributions are definitively settled month by month. There is no annual return, no annual true-up, no reconciliation filing and no year-end adjustment mechanism for health, pension, occupational risk, the solidarity fund or the payroll levies. Twelve monthly contribution returns are the complete annual record.
3154	What is required at or around year end:
3155	Obligation
3156	Timing
3157	Nature
3158	Severance-savings liquidation at 31 December and deposit to the employee's fund
3159	By 14 February
3160	Not a contribution — a statutory benefit deposit. Late deposit costs one day's salary per day of delay
3161	Interest on severance savings paid to the employee
3162	During January
3163	Paid to the employee, not to the fund
3164	Recalculation of the fixed withholding percentage (Procedure 2 only)
3165	December
3166	Tax, not social security
3167	Reparameterisation for the new minimum wage
3168	1 January
3169	The floor, ceiling and all triggers move with the new minimum wage
3170	Annual income and withholding certificate (Form 220) to each employee
3171	Last business day of March
3172	Tax
3173	
3174	Corrections, not adjustments. Where a past month was under- or over-declared, the fix is a correction planilla (planilla type N), in which the employer reports paired records — an A group carrying the originally settled value and a C group carrying the corrected value. The sum of the contribution bases in the C records must be greater than or equal to that of the A records, and likewise for the day counts. The correction may only be filed through the same information operator that carried the original return, absent an authorisation from the financial superintendence. Under-declaration attracts late-payment interest and UGPP assessment exposure.
3175	Legal basis: Law 100 of 1993 arts. 18, 20, 204 (as substituted by Law 797 of 2003 arts. 5 and 7, and Law 1122 of 2007 art. 10); Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30; Law 21 of 1982 arts. 9, 12, 17; Law 89 of 1988 art. 1; Law 2466 of 2025 arts. 34, 64; Tax Code arts. 114-1, 383 par. 2; Substantive Labour Code arts. 127, 128, 132, 143; Law 100 of 1993 art. 15 num. 2 (as amended by Law 797 of 2003 art. 3); Decree 1072 of 2015 arts. 2.2.4.2.5.4, 2.2.4.2.5.6, 2.2.4.2.5.7, 2.2.4.3.3, 2.2.4.3.4, 2.2.4.3.5, 2.2.4.3.6; Decree 1772 of 1994 art. 13; Decree 768 of 2022; Decree-Law 2090 of 2003; Decree 780 of 2016 arts. 3.2.1.5 and 3.2.2.1 (substituted by Decree 1990 of 2016 arts. 1 and 2); Resolution MSPS 2388 of 2016 and Technical Annex 2; Decree 0223 of 5 March 2026 art. 2.2.6.3.3.4; Law 50 of 1990 art. 99 num. 3; Law 52 of 1975 art. 1. Not yet in force, recorded for the 2027 change-control only: Law 2381 of 2024 arts. 19, 20, 21, 22, 28, 75, 94; Constitutional Court Auto 841 of 18 June 2025; Constitutional Court Judgment C-264 of 25 August 2026.
3176	5.3 Voluntary Contributions & Private Schemes
3177	Can employees and employers make additional voluntary contributions?
3178	Yes — through four distinct vehicles, each with its own tax character and its own cap. Distinguishing them correctly is essential, because two are non-taxable income and two are exempt income, and they consume different caps.
3179	#
3180	Vehicle
3181	Who contributes
3182	Tax character
3183	Cap
3184	1
3185	Voluntary contributions to the private individual pension account (cotizaciones voluntarias al RAIS)
3186	Employee, and employer on the employee's behalf
3187	Non-taxable income (Tax Code art. 55)
3188	25% of annual labour income and 2,500 tax units = COP 130,935,000
3189	2
3190	Voluntary pension fund contributions (aportes a fondos de pensiones voluntarias)
3191	Employee and employer
3192	Exempt income (Tax Code art. 126-1)
3193	Shared 30%-of-labour-income / 3,800 tax units = COP 199,021,200 annual cap
3194	3
3195	Construction-savings and long-term-savings accounts (AFC / AVC)
3196	Employee
3197	Exempt income (Tax Code art. 126-4)
3198	Shares the same 30% / 3,800-tax-unit cap with vehicle 2
3199	4
3200	Additional contributions to the public defined-benefit regime
3201	Employee
3202	Contribution, not a tax relief in itself
3203	Within the 25-minimum-wage ceiling
3204	
3205	Vehicle 1 — the individual-account voluntary contribution. Tax Code art. 55 treats these as ingresos no constitutivos de renta ni ganancia ocasional — non-taxable income deducted before the 40% and 1,340-tax-unit caps are applied, which makes them materially more valuable than exempt income. The cap is 25% of annual labour income, subject to an absolute 2,500 tax units. A withdrawal that does not meet the qualifying conditions is withheld at 35%.
3206	Vehicles 2 and 3 — voluntary pension funds and long-term savings accounts. Tax Code arts. 126-1 and 126-4 make these rentas exentas — exempt income deducted inside the 40% and 1,340-tax-unit caps, and sharing a single combined annual limit of the lower of 30% of labour income or 3,800 tax units. Employer contributions to a voluntary pension fund are deductible for the employer up to 3,800 tax units per employee (art. 126-1 first paragraph).
3207	Employer voluntary contributions to a voluntary pension fund on the employee's behalf are treated as the employee's own contribution for cap purposes and are exempt income in the employee's hands, within the same shared cap.
3208	Contingent withholding information duty. For every voluntary pension fund or AFC / AVC deposit made through payroll, the employer must inform the receiving entity, when the deposit is made, of the difference between the withholding that would have applied without the contribution and the withholding actually applied, computed on taxable labour income less the Tax Code art. 387 items (Decree 1625 of 2016 art. 1.2.4.1.28 for pension funds and art. 1.2.4.1.12 for AFC / AVC accounts, both as modified by Decree 2250 of 2017 art. 9). For its own voluntary contributions the employer must state in whose favour each contribution is made and the contingent-withholding amount, and deposit the contribution directly with the administrator (art. 1.2.1.22.42). The entity keeps the 'Retenciones Contingentes' control account (arts. 1.2.4.1.28 and 1.2.4.1.12) and withholds on a non-qualifying withdrawal (art. 1.2.4.1.37 for pension funds; arts. 1.2.4.1.35 and 1.2.4.1.36 for AFC / AVC). The decree prescribes no form; the channel and layout are set by each entity.
3209	There is no voluntary top-up of health, occupational risk or the payroll levies. Those three are fixed statutory percentages with no voluntary element.
3210	Which employee contributions are allowed as pre-tax deductions?
3211	Colombia does not use the term "pre-tax deduction". It uses two distinct legal categories, and the distinction determines where in the depuration each item sits.
3212	Category A — non-taxable income (deducted at step B, before the 40% cap):
3213	Item
3214	Rate or amount
3215	Source
3216	Mandatory pension contribution
3217	4% of the contribution base
3218	Tax Code art. 55
3219	Solidarity pension fund contribution
3220	1% + 0.2%–1.0%
3221	Tax Code art. 55 — confirmed by field 24 of the information return, which groups pension and solidarity contributions on one line
3222	Mandatory health contribution
3223	4% of the contribution base
3224	Tax Code art. 56
3225	Voluntary contributions to the individual pension account
3226	≤ 25% of annual labour income, cap 2,500 tax units
3227	Tax Code art. 55
3228	Third-party meal payments within the thresholds

3231

3233	Item

3235	Source
3236	Housing-loan interest and monetary correction
3237	100 tax units = COP 5,237,400
3238	Tax Code arts. 119 and 387

3251

3253	Item
3254	Amount
3255	Source
3256	Additional dependants deduction
3257	72 tax units per dependant per year, maximum 4 dependants = COP 3,770,928 each
3258	Tax Code art. 336 num. 3, second paragraph: "in addition to the limit established in the preceding paragraph"
3259	
3260	Not deductible against withholding. The 1% / 240-tax-unit invoice-based deduction of Tax Code art. 336 num. 5 is expressly excluded from the withholding calculation — "shall not be taken into account for the calculation of withholding at source" — confirmed by DIAN Concepto 015007 (int. 973) of 13 September 2023. It is available only in the employee's own annual return.
3261	Post-tax deductions with no tax effect: union dues, cooperative and savings-fund dues, payroll loans, insurance premiums other than qualifying health cover, and court-ordered garnishments.
3262	Are there private insurance schemes with employer and employee contributions?
3263	Yes. Four are common in the Colombian market. None is a statutory scheme, and none replaces a statutory contribution.
3264	Scheme
3265	Nature
3266	Employer contribution
3267	Employee contribution
3268	Tax treatment
3269	Prepaid medicine (medicina prepagada)
3270	Supplementary private health cover, on top of the compulsory health system
3271	Common — often 50% to 100%
3272	Common, by payroll deduction
3273	Employee may deduct up to 16 tax units per month (COP 837,984). The provider must be supervised by the National Health Superintendence
3274	Supplementary health plans (planes complementarios de salud)
3275	Sold by the employee's own health insurer
3276	Common
3277	Common
3278	Same 16-tax-unit monthly cap
3279	Group life and personal accident insurance
3280	Private cover, often extending occupational-risk benefits
3281	Common
3282	Sometimes
3283	Employer premiums are a deductible business expense; not a statutory employee deduction, and a taxable benefit to the employee where personal cover is provided
3284	Voluntary pension funds
3285	Third-pillar retirement savings, administered by the private pension administrators
3286	Common
3287	Common
3288	Exempt income within the shared 30% / 3,800-tax-unit annual cap
3289	
3290	Payroll treatment. Employer-paid premiums are generally structured as non-salary under a written Labour Code art. 128 pact, which keeps them out of the contribution base, the payroll-levy base and the statutory-benefit base — subject always to the 40% clamp. Employee-paid premiums are a post-tax payroll deduction requiring the employee's written authorisation for each case (Labour Code art. 149), which may separately generate a withholding deduction under Category B above.
3291	No occupational pension scheme exists in Colombia in the sense of a defined-benefit or defined contribution plan sponsored by the employer as an alternative to the state system. The mandatory pension system is universal, and the voluntary pension fund is an individual product to which an employer may contribute — not an employer-sponsored plan with its own trustees and rules.
