6379	Legal basis: Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 arts. 1.5.3.1.1, 1.5.3.2.1, 1.5.3.2.2, 1.5.3.3.1, 1.5.3.4.1.1, 1.5.3.5.1.7, 1.5.3.8.1, 1.5.3.8.2, 1.5.3.8.3, 1.5.3.8.5 and its Technical Annex; Resolution DIAN 000031 of 2024; Resolution DIAN 000022 of 20 February 2024; Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 arts. 1.3.7.1.1 and 1.3.9.1; Decree 1625 of 2016 arts. 1.6.1.13.2.33 and 1.6.1.13.2.40 (as set by Decree 2229 of 2023); Decree 780 of 2016 art. 3.2.2.1 (substituted by Decree 1990 of 2016 art. 2); Resolution MSPS 2388 of 2016 and Technical Annex 2; Law 21 of 1982 art. 17; Law 50 of 1990 art. 99; Law 52 of 1975 art. 1; Substantive Labour Code arts. 232, 306; Law 2466 of 2025 art. 15; DIAN Concepto 013840 int. 1568 of 1 October 2025.
6380	10.2 Integrations
6381	Is there any mandatory inbound integration?
6382	Yes — three, and they must not be defaulted to out of scope.
6383	#
6384	Inbound integration
6385	From
6386	Format
6387	What the engine must do with it
6388	1
6389	Validation response to the electronic payroll document (ApplicationResponse)
6390	DIAN, synchronously in the same SOAP call
6391	base64 XML
6392	Parse it. A document is legally issued only once validated. The engine must store the validation status and the CUNE, retry or correct rejections, and block the tax deduction of an unvalidated payroll cost. This is a hard inbound dependency, not a receipt
6393	2
6394	Contribution-return settlement response
6395	The licensed information operator
6396	Operator-specific file or portal output
6397	Reconcile the settled contributions against the engine's computation, capture the return number and payment date, and detect rejected contributor records
6398	3
6399	Health-insurer and occupational-risk benefit determinations
6400	Health insurer (EPS) and occupational-risk insurer (ARL)
6401	Portal output or file
6402	Determine the amount and period of the sickness, maternity, paternity and occupational-injury benefit actually recognised, so the employer's payroll advance can be reconciled and any shortfall recovered from the employee or absorbed
6403	
6404	Two further inbound flows that are operational rather than statutory but that a production implementation will need: the severance-savings fund's confirmation of the February deposit, and the payroll lender's instalment schedule under a libranza authorisation.
6405	Why this matters for the scope manifest. Inbound files that the engine must parse and act on carry product_mapping_required: true. Item 1 in particular is not optional — an employer that transmits without consuming the validation response cannot demonstrate that its payroll costs are supported, which is the entire purpose of the document.
6406	Is there any mandatory outbound integration beyond standard reporting?
6407	Yes — two, plus the payment file.
6408	#
6409	Outbound integration
6410	To
6411	Format
6412	Nature
6413	1
6414	Electronic payroll document transmission
6415	DIAN
6416	SOAP 1.2 SendNominaSync; base64 of a ZIP containing the XML; ≤ 500 KB; UTF-8; XAdES-EPES signature with an X.509 certificate from an accredited certification authority
6417	Genuine machine-to-machine. This goes beyond "standard reporting" — it is a real-time signed web-service call with a synchronous validation response
6418	2
6419	Graphical representation of the payroll document to the employee
6420	The employee
6421	PDF or printed
6422	Permissive under Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 art. 1.5.3.8.3 — but if issued, it must carry the art. 1.5.3.3.1 content and a QR code of at least 2 cm on every page
6423	3
6424	Bank payment file
6425	The employer's paying bank
6426	Bank-specific fixed-width or delimited format
6427	Colombia does not use IBAN. Domestic account numbers are numeric, of bank-specific length (commonly 10–17 digits), with no national check-digit standard. The bank-entity code catalogue is bank- and clearing-house-specific and must be obtained per client
6428	
6429	Third-party operation. Where Mercans generates or transmits the electronic payroll document on the client's behalf, Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 art. 1.5.3.8.1 requires that the provider be enabled by the tax authority, be registered in the tax registry, and that both parties retain the mandate document accrediting the arrangement. This is a contractual precondition to go-live, not a technical one.
6430	Ownership note (control B7). The following are explicitly owned upstream and are not Mercans deliverables: the licensed information operator's own file format and portal (owned by the operator); the severance-savings fund's deposit interface (owned by the fund); the payroll lender's schedule (owned by the lender); and the bank's payment-file specification (owned by the client's bank). Each implies an inbound data contract the engine must receive — respectively the settlement response, the deposit confirmation, the instalment schedule and the payment acknowledgement. Reversing any of these scope calls is a contained change to one interface, not a redesign.
6431	10.3 Payslip Requirements
6432	List all mandatory components that must be itemized on an employee payslip.
6433	The headline finding: Colombia has no statutory payslip-content article in its labour code. The Substantive Labour Code was grep-tested across its full consolidated text for the seven Spanish terms used for a payslip — desprendible, comprobante de pago, colilla, volante de pago, recibo de pago, constancia de pago and detalle de la liquidación — with zero hits. Law 2466 of 2025 added none.
6434	The operative itemisation rule is therefore tax law, not labour law: Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 article 1.5.3.3.1, which lists the mandatory content of the electronic payroll support document, combined with article 1.5.3.8.3, which makes the graphical representation permissive but requires it to carry that content if it is issued.
6435	Mandatory content — Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 art. 1.5.3.3.1 (13 items):
6436	#
6437	Item
6438	1
6439	Employer's or issuer's surnames and names, or corporate name, and tax identification number
6440	2
6441	Employee's or beneficiary's surnames and names, and identity document number
6442	3
6443	Identification of the electronic payroll support document — a unique consecutive number preceded by the authorised prefix
6444	4
6445	Generation date
6446	5
6447	Payment or credit date
6448	6
6449	Accrued payroll values (valores devengados de nómina), itemised by concept
6450	7
6451	Deducted payroll values (valores deducidos de nómina), itemised by concept
6452	8
6453	Total difference between accrued and deducted
6454	9
6455	Payment or credit period
6456	10
6457	Payment method and medium
6458	11
6459	Country, department and municipality of origin
6460	12
6461	Content of the authorised technical annex
6462	13
6463	CUNE — the unique electronic payroll code
6464	
6465	Additional mandatory formal requirements where a graphical representation is issued:
6466	Requirement
6467	Rule
6468	Language
6469	Spanish, mandatory. Another language may be added, never substituted (art. 26)
6470	Currency
6471	Colombian pesos, mandatory. Another currency may be added (art. 26)
6472	QR code
6473	At least 2 cm × 2 cm on every page, encoding the tax authority's document-search URL with the CUNE as the key (Technical Annex §13.1)
6474	Legal status
6475	The paper or PDF document is a representation of the electronic document, not the document itself. Its legal validity derives from the validated XML
6476	
6477	Mercans practice — items that are good practice but not legally required:
6478	the itemisation of employer contributions for transparency (never a deduction from the employee);
6479	the statutory benefit accruals and balances;
6480	the vacation balance;
6481	the hourly divisor and rest-day premium percentage applied, given the two mid-2026 cut-overs;
6482	the contract type, hire date and pay frequency;
6483	year-to-date totals.
6484	A formatting rule that must not be blended. The payslip carries the XML figures — two decimals, round-half-to-even. It must not display the contribution-return figures (rounded up to the nearest 100) as if they were the deduction, nor the Form 350 figure (rounded to the nearest 1,000). Where the client requires the contribution-return figure to be shown, it must be shown as a separate, labelled line.
6485	Legal basis: Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 arts. 1.5.3.3.1, 1.5.3.8.3, 1.5.3.8.5 and Technical Annex §13.1; Substantive Labour Code (Decree 2663 of 1950), full consolidated text — no payslip-content article.
6486	10.4 Other Compliance
6487	Is payroll software certification mandatory?
6488	Not certification in the formal sense — but enablement is mandatory, and it functions as a gate.
6489	Requirement
6490	Status
6491	Formal software certification or accreditation scheme
6492	No. There is no Colombian payroll-software certification body, no certificate of conformity and no periodic recertification
6493	Enablement (habilitación) of the issuer with the tax authority
6494	Mandatory. Global validation rule 92 rejects any document from a non-enabled issuer. The employer, or the third-party provider acting for it, must complete the enablement process in the tax authority's test environment before transmitting in production
6495	Software identifier and secret PIN
6496	Mandatory. Assigned by the tax authority at software activation. The PIN never appears in the XML; it feeds both the software security code and the CUNE
6497	Software security code
6498	Mandatory. SHA-384 of SoftwareID + PIN + document number
6499	Digital certificate
6500	Mandatory. X.509 from a certification authority accredited by the national accreditation body, used for the XAdES-EPES signature
6501	Third-party provider registration
6502	Mandatory where a provider generates or transmits. The provider must be enabled and registered in the tax registry, and both parties must retain the mandate document (art. 22)
6503	Contribution-return software
6504	No certification. Filing is through a licensed information operator, and the operator's technical guide governs the file it accepts
6505	
6506	Practical consequence. Enablement is a project prerequisite with a lead time, not a configuration step. It requires the employer's digital certificate, the tax-registry registration, and a successful pass through the test environment. It must be scheduled at the start of an implementation, not at go-live.
6507	Other compliance obligations that are not software-related:
6508	Obligation
6509	Source
6510	Overtime register — activity, total supplementary hours, day/night split, evidence of payment; available to employees and to administrative and judicial authorities
6511	Labour Code art. 162 num. 2 (Law 2466 of 2025 art. 12); Circular 101 of 2025 §3. Failure allows inspectors to suspend all supplementary work for six months
6512	Vacation register — entry date, start and end of each vacation, remuneration paid
6513	Labour Code art. 187 num. 3
6514	Internal work rules (reglamento de trabajo), published
6515	Labour Code art. 120, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 8
6516	Disciplinary due process before any sanction
6517	Labour Code art. 115, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 7
6518	Disability-hiring quota — employers with up to 500 employees must have at least two employees with a disability per 100; from 501 employees, one additional per 100-employee tranche, on the permanent headcount, with health-ministry certification. Contracts must be reported to the Ministry of Labour within 15 days of signature. The values are optional in the law's first year and mandatory from the second
6519	Law 2466 of 2025 art. 15
6520	Apprentice quota, or its monetisation
6521	Law 789 of 2002 art. 34, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 23
6522	Data-protection compliance — internal data-treatment policy, privacy notice, database registration
6523	Law 1581 of 2012; Decree 1074 of 2015
6524	
6525	10.5 Year-End Processing and Finalisation
6526	Describe the mandatory year-end finalisation process.
6527	Colombia's year end is a settlement and certification process, not a reconciliation. There is no annual tax true-up and no annual social-security reconciliation.
6528	DECEMBER 2026  1. Pay the SECOND HALF of the service bonus         by 20 December     = (salary + transport allowance) x days in the semester / 360  2. Deliver the third WORK-CLOTHING issue            by 20 December  3. RECALCULATE the fixed withholding percentage for every employee on     Procedure 2:  (sum of 12 months' taxable payments) / 13 -> table -> %     Binds January to June 2027.  4. LIQUIDATE SEVERANCE SAVINGS at 31 December     = (salary + transport allowance) x days of service in the year / 360     Base rule (art. 253): the LAST monthly salary if unchanged in the last     3 months; otherwise the average of the last year.  5. Compute the INTEREST on the 31 December balance     = balance x 12% x days / 360  6. Run the December payroll and transmit the December XML by 10 January.JANUARY 2027  7. PAY THE INTEREST ON SEVERANCE SAVINGS TO THE EMPLOYEE, during January.     Not to the fund - directly to the employee.     Failure = an indemnity equal to the interest owed, once only.  8. REPARAMETERISE: new minimum wage, new transport allowance, new tax unit.     Every floor, ceiling, trigger and band boundary moves.  9. File the December contribution return and Form 350 on the ordinary     business-day calendar.FEBRUARY 2027 10. DEPOSIT SEVERANCE SAVINGS to each employee's chosen fund     BEFORE 15 FEBRUARY (i.e. by 14 February).     Late deposit = ONE DAY'S SALARY FOR EACH DAY OF DELAY.     Exception: where the employer proves direct payment to the employee,     that payment is discharging and no penalty applies     (art. 57 par. 4, Law 2466 of 2025 art. 15) - but fund affiliation     remains compulsory.MARCH 2027 11. ISSUE FORM 220, the annual income and withholding certificate,     to EVERY employee, by the LAST BUSINESS DAY OF MARCH.MAY-JUNE 2027 12. File the INFORMATION RETURN, Format 2276 version 4, by the deadline     applicable to the employer's tax number.
6529	The most expensive year-end failure is the severance-savings deposit. One day's salary per day of delay accrues without cap until the deposit is made, and it is a per-employee penalty. For a workforce of 100 employees on the minimum wage, a two-week delay is roughly COP 81.7 million.

Where the employee is not required to file an annual return, art. 380 requires the certificate to carry the employee's own declarations of their other income, assets and liabilities, and it then substitutes for the employee's return

6560	On request
6561	The employee is expressly entitled to see it
6562	Labour Code art. 162 num. 2; Circular 101 of 2025 §3
6563	
6564	Is there a formal process for correcting errors discovered after the fiscal year has been closed?
6565	Yes — four separate processes, one per authority. There is no single "prior-year adjustment" mechanism.
6566	Error
6567	Correction route
6568	Time limit
6569	Withholding-tax return (Form 350) understated
6570	File a correction return increasing the declared value. Tax Code art. 588: within 3 years of the filing deadline and before a special requirement or statement of charges has been notified; a correction penalty applies
6571	3 years
6572	Withholding-tax return overstated
6573	Correction under Tax Code art. 589, which reduces the declared value. Must be filed within 1 year of the original filing deadline
6574	1 year
6575	Withholding practised in excess of, or otherwise than in accordance with, the law
6576	Decree 1625 of 2016 art. 1.2.4.16: on the employee's written request with evidence, the employer reimburses the excess and deducts the reimbursed amount from the withholdings of the following period, carrying any surplus forward until absorbed. The employer retains the employee's written request
6577	Ongoing
6578	Social-security contributions under-declared
6579	Correction planilla (type N): paired records, an A group carrying the originally settled values and a C group carrying the corrected values. The sum of the contribution bases and of the day counts in the C records must be greater than or equal to those in the A records. Filed only through the same information operator that carried the original return, absent an authorisation from the financial superintendence. Late-payment interest accrues from each original due date
6580	No statutory limit; the contributions authority's assessment powers run to 5 years
6581	Social-security contributions over-declared
6582	The correction planilla is structurally designed for under-declaration. A genuine overpayment requires a refund claim to each receiving entity — the health insurer, pension administrator, occupational-risk insurer, family compensation fund, apprenticeship service or family welfare institute — each with its own procedure
6583	Per entity
6584	Electronic payroll document wrong
6585	Adjustment note (NominaIndividualDeAjuste) — a replacement or cancellation document referencing the original by its unique identifier
6586	Per the annex
6587	Information return (Format 2276) wrong
6588	Correction return through the tax authority's information-return service. Penalties apply for late, incorrect or incomplete information under Tax Code art. 651
6589	
6590	Statutory benefits under-paid
6591	Pay the shortfall directly to the employee, with the applicable indemnity. The limitation period for the employee's claim is 3 years from the date the obligation became enforceable (Labour Code art. 488, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 62)
6592	3 years
6593	Severance savings deposited late or short
6594	Pay the shortfall plus one day's salary for each day of delay
6595	—
6596	
6597	Sequencing rule. Where a single error affects more than one authority — for example an omitted retrospective salary increase — correct in this order: (1) recompute the statutory benefits and pay the employee; (2) file the social-security correction planillas for each affected month with interest; (3) declare the tax effect in the current month's Form 350, since withholding tax follows the payment date; (4) issue an adjustment note for the electronic payroll document; (5) reissue Form 220 if the year has been certified.
6598	Legal basis: Tax Code (Decree-Law 624 of 1989) arts. 378, 379, 380, 386, 588, 589, 651, 850; Decree 1625 of 2016 arts. 1.2.4.16 and 1.6.1.13.2.40; Resolution DIAN 000022 of 20 February 2024; Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 (including arts. 1.5.3.3.1, 1.5.3.8.1, 1.5.3.8.3, 1.5.3.8.5); Substantive Labour Code arts. 57 (nums. 7 and par. 4), 65 par. 1, 115, 120, 162 num. 2, 187 num. 3, 232, 249, 253, 306, 488 (as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 arts. 7, 8, 12, 15, 62); Law 50 of 1990 art. 99; Law 52 of 1975 art. 1; Law 789 of 2002 art. 34 (as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 23); Resolution MSPS 2388 of 2016 Technical Annex 2; Law 1581 of 2012; Decree 1074 of 2015; Ministry of Labour Circular 101 of 2025 §3.
6599	
6600	Module 11: Advanced Scenarios & Cross-Cutting Logic
6601	Purpose: To capture complex, overarching rules that affect multiple aspects of payroll.
6602	11.1 Calculation Logic
6603	How is the proration and projection logic applied for income tax, social security, exemptions, and deductions?
6604	Colombia prorates on a 30-day commercial month and a 360-day year, and — because the system is neither cumulative nor annualised — it projects nothing.
6605	Element
6606	Proration
6607	Projection
6608	Basic salary
6609	S × days worked ÷ 30
6610	None
6611	Transport allowance
6612	249,095 × days worked ÷ 30
6613	None
6614	Contribution base
6615	On the prorated salary; the 1-minimum-wage floor is also prorated to the contributory days
6616	None
6617	Contribution-base ceiling
6618	25 minimum wages, applied to the monthly base — not prorated
6619	None
6620	Solidarity-fund trigger
6621	Tested on the monthly base as computed — not prorated, not annualised
6622	None
6623	Exemption test (art. 114-1)
6624	Tested on the month's actual earnings — not prorated to a full-month equivalent, not averaged
6625	None
6626	Payroll-levy base
6627	On the prorated salary plus paid rest days; no ceiling
6628	None
6629	Withholding tax, Procedure 1
6630	Art. 385 sub-30-day rule: (payments ÷ days) × 30 → read the table percentage → apply to the actual payments
6631	None — this is a grossing-up for rate determination, not a projection of future income
6632	Withholding tax, Procedure 2
6633	The fixed percentage applies to whatever the month's base is
6634	Backward-looking only — 12 months' history ÷ 13
6635	Annual exemption caps (790 and 1,340 tax units)
6636	Apportioned at one twelfth per month — 65.8333 units = COP 3,447,955 and 111.6667 units = COP 5,848,430
6637	None
6638	Per-dependant deduction (72 tax units per year, max 4)
6639	Apportioned at 6 tax units per dependant per month = COP 314,244
6640	None
6641	Monthly-capped deductions (housing interest 100 units, prepaid health 16 units, dependants 32 units)
6642	Already monthly — no proration
6643	None
6644	Statutory benefit accruals
6645	base × days ÷ 360 for the service bonus, severance savings and interest; base × days ÷ 720 for vacation
6646	None
6647	Vacation entitlement
6648	1.25 working days per month of service
6649	None
6650	
6651	The one genuine "projection-like" mechanic is Procedure 2, and it looks backwards. The fixed percentage is derived from the preceding 12 months' taxable payments divided by 13 — never from an estimate of the year ahead. There is no projected annual salary, no notional bonus and no year-end catch-up anywhere in Colombian payroll.
6652	A proration asymmetry worth stating. The contribution-base floor is prorated to the contributory days; the contribution-base ceiling is not. An employee hired on the 20th of a month contributes on 11 days at the prorated floor, but if that employee earns COP 60,000,000 for those 11 days the base is capped at the full monthly 25-minimum-wage ceiling of COP 43,772,625, not at 11/30 of it.
6653	If tax or contribution rates change mid-pay period, which rates apply?
6654	Colombia's default rule is that a statutory change applies from its stated effective date, prospectively, and a pay period spanning the date must be split. Colombian statutes are unusually explicit about effective dates, and 2026 has three.
6655	Change
6656	Effective date
6657	Rule
6658	Minimum wage
6659	1 January
6660	Applies from 1 January. Every derived floor, ceiling and trigger moves at once. There is no transitional or blended rate
6661	Tax unit
6662	1 January
6663	Applies to the whole tax year. Every band boundary and cap reprices
6664	Rest-day and holiday premium: 80% → 90%
6665	1 July 2026
6666	Split the month. Hours worked on rest days up to 30 June carry 80%; from 1 July, 90%. Circular 101 of 2025 §4
6667	Hourly divisor: 220 → 210
6668	15 July 2026
6669	Split the month. The ordinary hour value to 14 July is salary ÷ 220; from 15 July it is salary ÷ 210 — a rise of about 4.8% in every premium. Circular 101 of 2025
6670	Night window: 21:00 → 19:00
6671	25 December 2025
6672	Already fully effective for all of 2026
6673	Maximum ordinary week: 44 → 42 hours
6674	15 July 2026
6675	Split at 15 July. Maximum ordinary plus overtime hours falls from 56 to 54 per week
6676	Contribution rates
6677	No change for 2026
6678	—
6679	Occupational-risk class or rate
6680	On reclassification
6681	Applies from the effective date of the reclassification; report a work-centre change event
6682	Employee-requested higher withholding rate
6683	The month after the request (Tax Code art. 383 par. 3)
6684	Never mid-month
6685	Procedure 2 fixed percentage
6686	1 July and 1 January
6687	Never mid-semester
6688	
6689	July 2026 is the critical month, and it has two cut-overs two weeks apart. They must not be collapsed:
6690	1 - 30 June 2026     rest-day premium 80%,  divisor 2201 - 14 July 2026     rest-day premium 90%,  divisor 22015 - 31 July 2026    rest-day premium 90%,  divisor 210
6691	A rest-day hour worked on 10 July 2026 by an employee on COP 2,400,000 is therefore 2,400,000 ÷ 220 × 0.90 = COP 9,818.18 of premium, while the same hour on 20 July is 2,400,000 ÷ 210 × 0.90 = COP 10,285.71. Applying one rate to the whole month understates or overstates every premium hour in it.
6692	The tax-authority position on reporting the split. DIAN Concepto 013840 (int. 1568) of 1 October 2025 confirms that "the possible coexistence of differentiated premium percentages within the same month does not constitute any obstacle to the generation, transmission and subsequent validation" of the electronic payroll document, and that the annex's published percentage "must be read in accordance with the rules in force for each specific case". The employer consolidates all the month's earnings, each premium settled at the percentage applicable to its own date.
6693	How are tax and social security handled when an employee is paid multiple times in a single pay period?
6694	Both are monthly by construction, so multiple payments are aggregated into one monthly calculation.
6695	Aspect
6696	Rule
6697	Social security
6698	One contribution base per employee per month, built from all payments attributed to the month. One set of contributions. One record on the contribution return. Floors, ceilings and triggers all tested on the aggregate
6699	Withholding tax, Procedure 1
6700	The table is read on the month's depurated total. Where the employer withholds on each payment, the second withholding must be computed as table(month total) − already withheld, not as a fresh read on the second payment alone. Reading the table twice on two half-month bases systematically under-withholds, because the progressive bands are applied twice from zero
6701	Withholding tax, Procedure 2
6702	The fixed percentage is applied to each payment, or to the month's total; both give the same result, because the percentage is linear
6703	Electronic payroll XML
6704	One document per employee per month, aggregating all payments — Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 art. 1.5.3.2.2 and DIAN Concepto 013840 of 2025
6705	Form 350
6706	One monthly aggregate
6707	The statutory service bonus under Procedure 1
6708	Keeps its separate table read even if paid in the same month as salary
6709	Severance savings and interest
6710	Excluded from the monthly base under both procedures, whenever paid
6711	
6712	The dominant Colombian case is the semi-monthly payroll. Market practice pays the 15th as a fixed advance of half the basic salary with no withholding, and settles the whole month on the second payment: all variable pay, the whole month's contributions and the whole month's withholding. This is arithmetically correct and is what the engine should default to. The statutorily rigorous alternative — grossing each half-month to 30 days under art. 385, reading the table percentage and applying it to the actual half-month payment — produces a smoother net and is available where a client requires it.
6713	Off-cycle payments in the same month (a bonus paid on the 20th, a termination settlement paid on the 25th) are aggregated into the same monthly base for both tax and social security. The tax effect of an additional payment is therefore always table(new month total) − already withheld.
6714	How is payroll calculated if an employee's pay cycle changes mid-year?
6715	A change of pay frequency is an administrative change, not a statutory event. Colombian law neither restricts nor regulates it, beyond the outer limits of Labour Code art. 134 — one week for day wages, one month for salaries — and the general requirement that salary be paid in equal periods and in arrears.
6716	Aspect
6717	Effect of a frequency change
6718	Contribution rates, floors, ceilings, triggers
6719	None — all are monthly parameters
6720	Contribution return
6721	None — monthly regardless
6722	Electronic payroll XML
6723	None — monthly regardless. Only the pay-frequency code changes
6724	Withholding table
6725	None — the table is monthly
6726	Procedure 1
6727	The art. 385 sub-30-day rule applies to any transitional period shorter than a month
6728	Procedure 2
6729	None — the fixed percentage is unaffected. The 12-month accumulator simply continues
6730	Statutory benefit accruals
6731	None — they accrue on time, not on payments
6732	Vacation accrual
6733	None
6734	
6735	Procedure for a mid-year change.
6736	1. Change at a MONTH BOUNDARY. Both statutory artefacts are monthly and   keyed to the payment month; a mid-month change splits an indivisible filing.2. Ensure no PERIOD IS SKIPPED OR DOUBLE-PAID at the transition.   Monthly -> semi-monthly:  pay the full month on the last day, then start                             the 15th/last-day pattern in the following month.   Semi-monthly -> monthly:  pay the second half as normal, then move to a                             single month-end payment.   Weekly -> semi-monthly:   pay the outstanding weeks to the changeover date,                             then start the new pattern. A transitional period                             shorter than 30 days uses the art. 385 rule.3. Update the PAY-FREQUENCY CODE on the employee record for the XML.4. Preserve every ACCUMULATOR: 12-month taxable payments, 6-month average   labour income, year-to-date cap consumption, benefit accruals, vacation   balance. None of them resets.5. Where the contract specifies the pay date, obtain the employee's WRITTEN   AGREEMENT to the change. A unilateral change to a contractual pay date is   a variation of an essential term and, if adverse, is capable of amounting   to indirect dismissal under Labour Code art. 62 lit. B).6. Do NOT extend the interval beyond the art. 134 maxima: one week for   day wages, one month for salaries.
6737	The one real risk is a gap or an overlap at the transition, which produces either an unpaid period — exposing the employer to the Labour Code art. 65 late-payment penalty — or a double payment that must be recovered under art. 151, requiring a written agreement with the instalment and amortisation period stated.
6738	Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 62, 65, 127, 134, 151, 179 (as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 14), 161 (as substituted by art. 11); Law 2101 of 2021 arts. 3 and 4; Ministry of Labour Circular 101 of 22 September 2025 §§2.1, 4, 5.1; Tax Code arts. 383 par. 3, 385, 386, 388, 114-1; Law 100 of 1993 art. 18; Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 art. 1.5.3.2.2; DIAN Concepto 013840 int. 1568 of 1 October 2025; Resolution DIAN 000238 of 2025; Decree 1469 of 2025.
6739	11.2 Special Employee Groups & Income
6740	How does taxation and social security differ for part-time employees and Board of Directors?
6741	Part-time employees.
6742	Colombia has no distinct part-time employment regime, no separate part-time contract type and no reduced part-time entitlements. A part-time employee is an ordinary employee whose agreed hours are fewer than the maximum.
6743	Aspect
6744	Part-time treatment
6745	Minimum wage
6746	Applies proportionally to time worked. There is no separate part-time rate
6747	Contribution rates
6748	Identical — health 4%/8.5%, pension 4%/12%, occupational risk, levies 4%/2%/3%
6749	Contribution base
6750	Actual earnings, but floored at 1 minimum wage where the employee works a full month on part-time hours. This is the defining part-time cost effect: an employee earning COP 900,000 for a full month of part-time work still contributes on COP 1,750,905
6751	Statutory benefits
6752	Full proportional entitlement — service bonus, severance savings, interest, vacation, work clothing
6753	Paid weekly rest
6754	Labour Code art. 173 num. 5: where the agreed schedule does not require work on all working days of the week, the rest day is paid in proportion to time worked
6755	Withholding tax
6756	Ordinary. In practice a part-time employee is almost always in the 0% band
6757	Overtime
6758	Hours beyond the agreed ordinary day are supplementary work, even where they are below the statutory maximum (Labour Code art. 159)
6759	Part-time contributions for micro-enterprises and households
6760	Law 2466 of 2025 art. 34 permits micro-enterprises and households to pay social security on a part-time basis, computed in weeks and settled monthly, and to contribute proportionally where monthly income is below the minimum wage. Requires commercial registration, accounting books and an internal contract register. Monitored by the contributions authority; may not be used where the work is in fact full-time (par. 2). Outside standard corporate payroll scope
6761	
6762	Board of Directors. The treatment turns on the substance of the relationship, not the title.
6763	Situation
6764	Employment?
6765	Social security
6766	Withholding tax
6767	Executive director holding an employment contract
6768	Yes
6769	Full employee treatment on the whole remuneration — salary and any director's fees that are consideration for the service. Employer 12% + 8.5% + occupational risk + levies; employee 4% + 4% + solidarity fund
6770	Art. 383 progressive table
6771	Non-executive director receiving attendance fees only
6772	No — a corporate mandate, not employment
6773	Not an employee contribution. The director contributes as an independent worker on a base of 40% of the monthly fee, floored at 1 minimum wage (operating practice; legal basis to be confirmed)
6774	Art. 383 table still applies — art. 383 par. 2, added by Law 2277 of 2022 art. 8, extends the employment table to non-employment work income using "pagos mensualizados": total contract value less mandatory health and pension contributions, divided by the months of the contract
6775	Statutory legal representative on an employment contract
6776	Yes
6777	Full employee treatment. Generally excluded from the maximum-hours rules as management staff (Labour Code art. 162 lit. a)
6778	Art. 383 table
6779	
6780	Two engine consequences. A person who is both an employee and a fee-earning director has two different social-security bases — full salary for the employment, 40% of the fees for the mandate — but one withholding table, and the two accumulate against the shared 25-minimum-wage ceiling. A fee-only director is outside Mercans payroll scope and is handled as an independent-contractor payment; the client retains that responsibility.
6781	How is taxation handled for employees with multiple employers?
6782	Social security is the constrained side; tax is not.
6783	Aspect
6784	Rule
6785	Social security — the shared ceiling
6786	Law 100 of 1993 art. 18 par. 1: where the employee has two or more employers, contributions are made proportionally to each remuneration and accumulate up to the legal ceiling of 25 minimum wages. Critically: "contributions to the health system must be made on the same base" — the health base must match the pension base
6787	How the proportion works
6788	Each employer contributes on its own remuneration. Where the combined base would exceed 25 minimum wages, the ceiling is allocated pro rata to each employer's share of the total remuneration. Each employer therefore needs the other employer's base, which only the employee can supply
6789	The employee's duty
6790	To declare the second employment and the base with the other employer. Without that declaration each employer applies the ceiling to its own base alone, and the employee over-contributes
6791	Occupational risk
6792	Contributed by each employer on its own base; there is no shared ceiling logic in practice, because each employer insures its own exposure at its own work centre
6793	Payroll levies
6794	Each employer on its own payroll-levy base; no ceiling at all, so no allocation question arises
6795	Health and pension institutions
6796	The employee has one health insurer and one pension administrator. Both employers pay to the same institutions
6797	Withholding tax
6798	Each employer withholds independently, on its own payments, as if it were the only employer. There is no aggregation, no cumulative code, no coordination and no employer duty to enquire
6799	The consequence
6800	Each employer applies the 0% band, the 25% exemption and the art. 387 deductions to its own payments. The employee is therefore systematically under-withheld across the two employments, and reconciles — usually with a balance to pay — on the annual income-tax return
6801	The employee's remedy
6802	Tax Code art. 383 par. 3: request a higher withholding rate in writing from one or both employers, effective from the month after the request. This is the intended mechanism and the engine must support it
6803	Exemption test (art. 114-1)
6804	Applied by each employer separately on the remuneration it pays. An employee earning 6 minimum wages from each of two employers is below 10 minimum wages at each, so both employers are exonerated, even though the employee earns 12 minimum wages in total
6805	
6806	Configuration. Carry a multi-employer flag and, where declared, the other employer's contribution base, so that the ceiling allocation can be computed. Do not attempt to aggregate for tax.
6807	What is the treatment of foreign-sourced employment income for residents?
6808	A Colombian tax resident is taxed on worldwide income (Tax Code art. 9), so foreign-sourced employment income is taxable in Colombia. What matters for payroll is who pays it.
6809	Situation
6810	Payroll treatment
6811	Resident employee, paid by the Colombian employer for work performed abroad
6812	Ordinary Colombian payroll. Fully taxable under the art. 383 table, fully contributory. The place of performance does not change the treatment of a payment made by a Colombian employer to a resident
6813	Resident employee, paid directly by a foreign group company (a "shadow payroll" or split-payroll arrangement)
6814	The Colombian employer is a withholding agent only in respect of payments it makes. A payment made by a foreign entity with no Colombian withholding-agent status is not subject to Colombian withholding. The income remains taxable to the employee, who declares it in the annual return. Colombia has no shadow-payroll obligation requiring the local entity to withhold on foreign-paid amounts, unless the local entity in substance bears the cost, in which case the payment is treated as made by it
6815	Resident employee with pre-arrival foreign employment income in the year of arrival
6816	Residence attaches to the whole tax year in which the 183rd day falls, so pre-arrival income of that year is within the worldwide base. Not a payroll matter; the employee declares it
6817	Foreign social-security contributions paid by a resident
6818	Not deductible against the Colombian withholding base. Only Colombian mandatory pension, solidarity-fund and health contributions are non-taxable income under arts. 55 and 56
6819	Double-tax relief
6820	Tax Code art. 254 grants a credit for tax paid abroad on foreign-sourced income, limited to the Colombian tax on the same income. Claimed in the employee's annual return, not through payroll
6821	Treaty relief
6822	Colombia's treaty network and Andean Community Decision 578 may assign the taxing right to the other state. Applied only on written client instruction supported by a residence certificate
6823	
6824	The rule of thumb for the engine: withhold on what the Colombian entity pays; report what it pays; do not attempt to gross up or shadow-withhold on foreign-paid amounts unless the client instructs it and the local entity bears the cost.
6825	How do employer stock option plans work and how are they taxed?
6826	Colombia has no dedicated statutory regime for employee share schemes — no approved-plan legislation, no tax-advantaged option scheme and no specific valuation rule. The treatment is derived from first principles.
6827	Aspect
6828	Treatment
6829	Legal nature
6830	A benefit derived from the employment relationship. Where it is ordinary and permanent consideration for the service it is salary in kind (Labour Code art. 129); where it is occasional or gratuitous, or subject to a valid written non-salary pact, it is not salary (art. 128)
6831	Taxable event
6832	Exercise (for options) or vesting (for restricted shares) — the point at which the employee acquires an unconditional economic benefit
6833	Taxable amount
6834	The spread: market value of the shares at exercise or vesting, less any exercise price paid by the employee
6835	Tax character
6836	Employment income, taxable within the ordinary monthly base under the art. 383 progressive table. There is no preferential rate
6837	Withholding
6838	The employer withholds. Where the benefit is non-cash and cash pay is insufficient, the minimum-wage protection rules of Module 4.1 apply and the shortfall is carried
6839	Social security
6840	Contributory only if the award is salary. Where a valid written non-salary pact exists, it is out of the contribution base up to the 40% clamp; the excess above 40% of total remuneration goes back in
6841	Statutory benefits
6842	Enter the service-bonus, severance-savings and vacation bases only if salary
6843	Subsequent disposal
6844	A separate transaction for the employee — occasional gain or capital income depending on the holding period. Outside payroll
6845	Awards from a foreign parent
6846	The Colombian entity withholds where it makes or bears the cost of the payment. Where the foreign parent grants and settles directly at its own cost, there is no Colombian withholding agent for that payment, but the income remains taxable to a resident employee, who declares it
6847	Non-resident employee
6848	Flat 20% under art. 408 where the underlying service was performed in Colombia; foreign-source and not taxable where it was performed abroad
6849	Valuation
6850	No statutory scale. Market value at the taxable event, evidenced by the plan administrator's valuation or the quoted price
6851	
6852	Configuration. Model an equity award as a taxable earnings line carrying the spread, with an explicit salary/non-salary flag, plus an offsetting deduction line where no cash is delivered. The salary/non-salary flag decides the contribution and benefit consequences; it has no tax effect.
6853	How is payroll handled for employees who transfer between different legal entities mid-year?
6854	The controlling concept is substitution of employer (sustitución patronal), Labour Code arts. 67–72, which distinguishes sharply from a resignation-and-rehire.
6855	Case A — substitution of employer (business transfer, merger, spin-off).
6856	Aspect
6857	Rule
6858	Definition — art. 67
6859	A change of employer "by virtue of an act between living persons (inter vivos)", with continuity of the business and of the employee in their post
6860	Effect on the contract — art. 68
6861	The employment contract is not terminated, suspended or modified. Seniority, entitlements and accruals continue unbroken
6862	Liability — arts. 69–70
6863	The outgoing and incoming employers are jointly and severally liable for obligations accrued before the substitution; the incoming employer alone is liable for those accruing after. They may agree between themselves on the internal allocation, without prejudice to the employee
6864	Statutory benefits
6865	Not settled at the transfer. Seniority runs continuously; the incoming employer inherits the accrued liability. The parties may agree to settle severance savings at the transfer date, with the employee's agreement
6866	Social security
6867	The incoming employer reports a hire change event and the outgoing employer a termination change event in the transfer month, under their different tax numbers. Contributions are split by days
6868	Withholding tax
6869	Each entity withholds independently on its own payments. There is no carry-over of year-to-date figures. Under Procedure 2 the incoming entity has no 12-month history of its own, so art. 386 directs it to read the table on the month's own payments until its first semi-annual calculation. Each entity issues its own Form 220 for the part-year it paid
6870	Annual caps
6871	Because each entity withholds independently, each applies the annual caps from zero. The employee may therefore over-consume the 790-unit and 1,340-unit caps across the two entities and reconcile in the annual return
6872	Electronic payroll XML
6873	Each entity issues its own document under its own tax number and enablement, for the part-month it paid
6874	Occupational risk
6875	Re-affiliation to the incoming employer's insurer; the risk class follows the incoming employer's work centre
6876	Health insurer, pension administrator, severance-savings fund
6877	Unchanged — these are employee elections and survive the transfer
6878	
6879	Case B — resignation and rehire within a group (no business continuity). This is a genuine termination and a genuine new hire:
6880	Full termination settlement by the outgoing entity — salary, severance savings, interest, service bonus,
6881	unused vacation. No dismissal indemnity where the employee resigns.
6882	Social-security payment evidence for the last three months, within 60 days, failing which the
6883	termination does not take effect (art. 65 par. 1).
6884	Seniority restarts at the incoming entity, with all the consequences for the indemnity tier, the
6885	work-clothing qualification and the vacation accumulation limits.
6886	Where the arrangement is in substance a device to break seniority, it is exposed to challenge —
6887	Labour Code art. 59A, added by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 48, prohibits employer manoeuvres of evasion (maniobras de elusión).
6888	The engine must carry a "continuity of service" flag distinguishing the two cases, because it drives seniority, the indemnity tier and whether benefits are settled at the transfer.
6889	What is the process for an employee who changes citizenship or residency status during the fiscal year?
6890	Tax residence change — the operationally significant case.
6891	Direction
6892	Trigger
6893	Payroll effect
6894	Non-resident → resident
6895	The employee is present for more than 183 days (from the 184th day), counting the days of entry and exit, in any 365-day period (Tax Code art. 10 num. 1); where the period straddles two years, residence attaches to the second year (DIAN Concepto 011108 of 29 June 2026, item 13)
6896	Switch from the flat 20% of art. 408 to the art. 383 progressive table, from the payment following the date the test is met. Confirmed by DIAN Concepto 011108 of 29 June 2026, which rejected withholding under art. 383 from day one in anticipation of residence: status is determined "at the time of each payment". No retroactive adjustment through payroll; the employee reconciles in the annual return
6897	Resident → non-resident
6898	Departure such that the 183-day test is no longer met for the relevant period
6899	Switch to the flat 20% for Colombian-source payments; no withholding on payments for services performed entirely outside Colombia (DIAN Oficio 28380 of 2019)
6900	
6901	Configuration requirements.
6902	A residence flag with an effective date on the employee record.
6903	Re-evaluation every payroll period, not annually.
6904	A presence-day accumulator rolling over any 365-day window — the test is not calendar-year based.
6905	A prospective-only switch. The engine must never restate prior months.
6906	Where the year straddles the threshold, residence attaches to the year in which the 183rd day falls.
6907	Citizenship change — far less consequential. Colombian nationality does not by itself determine tax residence for the presence test, and it does not change any contribution rate. What it changes:
6908	Aspect
6909	Effect
6910	Identity document
6911	From a foreigner identity card (code 22) or permit to a citizen identity card (code 13), with a new document number. This is a master-data change that must be reported as a change event on the contribution return and must be reflected in the electronic payroll XML and the annual certificate
6912	Foreign pension-system voluntary affiliation
6913	A foreign national who had not exercised the voluntary pension affiliation of Law 100 of 1993 art. 15 num. 2 (as amended by Law 797 of 2003 art. 3) should be reviewed on naturalisation
6914	Tax residence
6915	Article 10's nationality-plus-connection tests become available. A Colombian national is resident where the spouse, dependants, 50%+ of assets, 50%+ of income or the place of asset administration is in Colombia — tests that do not apply to a foreign national
6916	Contribution rates
6917	None
6918	
6919	The migration-status case, which is more common in practice. A change from a Special Permanence Permit (code 47) or a Temporary Protection Permit to a foreigner identity card (code 22) is also a document-number change requiring the same reporting. Note the open item recorded in Module 2.2: the electronic-payroll annex has no code for the Temporary Protection Permit, and code 47 is the only available proxy.
6920	Are there specific rules for expatriates or inpatriates?
6921	Colombia has no expatriate tax regime, no inpatriate concession and no impatriate relief. There is no reduced rate, no exempt allowance, no tax-free housing or schooling benefit, and no time-limited concession for inbound assignees. Expatriates are taxed under the ordinary rules, with residence as the only switch.
6922	Aspect
6923	Rule for an inbound assignee
6924	Labour law
6925	Fully applicable. Labour Code art. 2 applies the Code to all work performed in Colombian territory, whatever the employee's nationality. Law 2466 of 2025 art. 37: "Immigration status shall not be an impediment to claiming labour guarantees" — migration status is no bar to labour rights
6926	Withholding tax
6927	Flat 20% under art. 408 until residence is acquired; the art. 383 table thereafter. See Module 6
6928	Health and occupational risk
6929	Compulsory, with no waiver
6930	Pension
6931	Compulsory, except that, for a foreign employee not covered by a pension regime of the country of origin or of any other, affiliation is voluntary — Law 100 of 1993 art. 15 num. 2 (as amended by Law 797 of 2003 art. 3)
6932	Totalisation agreements
6933	Colombia has bilateral social-security agreements with Spain, Chile, Uruguay, Argentina, Ecuador and Peru, and is party to the Ibero-American Multilateral Social Security Convention. A posted worker with a certificate of coverage may remain covered at home for the certified period. Colombian occupational-risk cover is nonetheless required for work performed in Colombia
6934	Double-tax treaties
6935	Applied only on written client instruction with a residence certificate
6936	Expatriate allowances — housing, schooling, cost-of-living, hardship, home leave
6937	Fully taxable. No exemption exists. They are salary unless a valid written non-salary pact under Labour Code art. 128 applies, in which case they are out of the contribution base up to the 40% clamp
6938	Tax equalisation and gross-up
6939	Permitted contractually. The grossed-up amount is itself taxable income, so the calculation is iterative. No statutory recognition — a purely commercial arrangement
6940	Split payroll
6941	The Colombian entity withholds only on what it pays or bears. See the foreign-sourced income question above
6942	Work permit and visa
6943	A precondition of lawful employment, not a payroll parameter, but the visa type drives the available identity-document code
6944	Vacation accumulation
6945	Labour Code art. 190 allows 4 years' accumulation for "foreign nationals who render their services in places other than those of their family members' residence" — one of the few express expatriate provisions in Colombian law
6946	Termination
6947	Identical to a Colombian national. All statutory benefits and indemnities apply in full
6948	Transnational teleworkers
6949	Law 2466 of 2025 art. 54: a contract concluded in Colombia is governed by Colombian law, and the occupational-risk insurer must guarantee occupational cover in the country where the service is performed, with additional foreign insurance at the employer's cost where needed
6950	
6951	The single largest expatriate configuration risk remains the residence switch. A COP 15,000,000 salary produces COP 3,000,000 of withholding as a non-resident and COP 1,190,532 as a resident — a 2.5-fold difference that turns on one date. See the worked example in Module 6.3.
6952	Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 2, 59A, 62, 67, 68, 69, 70, 71, 72, 128, 129, 159, 162, 173 num. 5, 190 (art. 59A added by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 48); Law 100 of 1993 art. 18 par. 1; Law 2466 of 2025 arts. 34, 37, 45, 48, 54; Tax Code (Decree-Law 624 of 1989) arts. 9, 10, 55, 56, 114-1, 254, 383 (pars. 2 and 3), 386, 388, 408; Law 2277 of 2022 art. 8; Law 100 of 1993 art. 15 num. 2 (as amended by Law 797 of 2003 art. 3); DIAN Concepto 011108 of 29 June 2026; DIAN Oficio 28380 of 2019; Andean Community Decision 578; the Ibero-American Multilateral Social Security Convention.

7279	The lesson. The part-time employer cost factor is 1.4316 against 1.3579 for the full-time minimum-wage employee, purely because of the contribution-base floor. 7357	The ordering effect made visible. Had the 25% exemption been computed on the pre-deduction figure — 25% × 10,920,000 = 2,730,000 — the total deductions would have been 4,730,000, capped at 4,368,000, giving a base of 6,552,000 = 125.1002 tax units and a withholding of COP 299,529.30. The correct ordering produces COP 325,749.30. The difference of COP 26,220.00 per month is the cost of the ordering error, and it compounds across a workforce.

7378	Note on the class of relief. The voluntary pension and long-term savings contributions are exempt income (Tax Code arts. 126-1 and 126-4), deducted inside the 40% and 1,340-unit caps. Had the same COP 2,000,000 been paid instead as a voluntary contribution to the individual pension account under art. 55, it would have been non-taxable income, deducted before the cap reference figure was struck — a structurally more valuable relief. The engine must carry the two vehicles as distinct wage types.
7379	Example 4 — Employee with a student loan and a special statutory deduction
7380	Facts. Salary COP 3,500,000 — just below the 2-minimum-wage transport-allowance ceiling of COP 3,501,810, so the allowance is due. The employee repays a national student loan through payroll under a written payroll-lending authorisation at COP 350,000 per month, and is subject to the Higher Education Solidarity Contribution (Contribución Solidaria a la Educación Superior — Contribución Sabes) notified at COP 120,000 per month.
7381	Scope note. Colombia has no statutory student-loan repayment tax and no income-contingent repayment scheme deducted at source as a tax. The two Colombian analogues are (a) repayment of a national student loan through a payroll-lending authorisation under Law 1527 of 2012 — a voluntary, employee-authorised deduction, and (b) the Higher Education Solidarity Contribution, which Labour Code art. 150, as substituted by Law 1911 of 2018 art. 22, expressly makes a permitted deduction requiring no individual written order. Neither is a tax and neither reduces the withholding base.

7541	The lesson. Neither the student-loan repayment nor the Higher Education Solidarity Contribution touches the withholding base or the contribution base. Both are post-tax deductions, and both are bounded by the minimum-wage floor and the payroll-lending 50% rule. Note also how close this salary sits to the transport-allowance cliff: at COP 3,501,811 the allowance would be lost entirely, reducing gross by COP 249,095 and the statutory-benefit base with it.
7542	Example 5 — Complex scenario: all-inclusive salary above the exemption threshold
7543	Facts. Senior executive on an all-inclusive salary (salario integral) of COP 25,000,000 per month under a written agreement, indefinite-term contract, occupational risk class I. This is the most structurally distinctive Colombian remuneration form, and it engages four separate rules at once.

7602	Step 4 — The exemption test
7603	The employee earns COP 25,000,000, which is at or above 10 minimum wages (COP 17,509,050). The Tax Code art. 114-1 exemption is therefore LOST. Note carefully that the test is applied to what the employee earns — the full COP 25,000,000 — not to the 70% contribution base of COP 17,500,000, which would (barely) have fallen below the threshold. Testing on the contribution base would wrongly exonerate this employee and understate employer cost by roughly COP 2.36 million a month.

7719	The four lessons this example carries.
7720	The 70% rule applies to the contribution base and the payroll-levy base, not to tax. The whole
7721	COP 25,000,000 is taxable.
7722	The exemption test is on what the employee earns, not on the contribution base. Testing on the 70%
7723	figure would have wrongly exonerated the employee.
7724	Vacation is not absorbed and is owed on the full COP 25,000,000, while contributions run on 70%.
7725	Three different bases in one employee: 25,000,000 for tax and vacation, 17,500,000 for contributions and levies.
7726	The employer cost factor is lower — 1.2518 against 1.3579 for a minimum-wage employee — because the
7727	70% rule and the absorption of three of the four statutory benefits outweigh the loss of the exemption. This is why the all-inclusive salary is the standard senior-executive structure in Colombia.
7728	The 13-minimum-wage floor and the 70%-then-ceiling sequence, extended. Labour Code art. 132 num. 2 (as substituted by Law 50 de 1990 art. 18) sets the minimum all-inclusive salary at 10 minimum wages of ordinary salary PLUS a factor of not less than 30% of that amount -- 13 minimum wages in total, COP 22,761,765 for 2026. Below 13 minimum wages the integral-salary stipulation does not meet art. 132 num. 2, so it cannot validly absorb statutory benefits, surcharges and premiums. The worked example above never tests the 25-minimum-wage ceiling because 70% of COP 25,000,000 (17,500,000) sits well below it. The sequence matters, so it is worth making explicit with a number that actually binds: for the SAME executive on an all-inclusive salary of COP 70,000,000, 70% is 49,000,000 -- ABOVE the 25-minimum-wage ceiling of 43,772,625. The 70% factor is applied FIRST, to the full all-inclusive salary; the ceiling is then tested against THAT result, not against some pre-adjusted figure -- so the contribution and payroll-levy base for this employee is capped at 43,772,625, not at 70% of some ceiling-adjusted salary. The withholding-tax and vacation-accrual treatment do not change: both still run on the full COP 70,000,000, exactly as in steps 4 to 6 above, since the ceiling is a social-security-contribution rule only. The statute's own sequence confirms this: Ley 100 de 1993 art. 18, as rewritten by Ley 797 de 2003 art. 5, states the rules in this order within the SAME article -- (i) the base is the monthly salary, (ii) "el limite de la base de cotizacion sera de 25 salarios minimos", THEN, as the article's next and final substantive rule, (iii) "las cotizaciones de los trabajadores cuya remuneracion se pacte bajo la modalidad de salario integral, se calculara sobre el 70% de dicho salario." Rule (iii) computes what "la base de cotizacion" IS for an integral-salary worker; that resulting figure is then the thing tested against the ceiling already stated in rule (ii) -- the statute never gives a separate, pre-adjusted ceiling for salario integral. This follows from the order the amending article itself uses. Ley 2381 de 2024 art. 22 restates the same 25-minimum-wage ceiling and the same 70% rule.

7779	Note on example 3: net pay is after the employee's own COP 2,000,000 of voluntary savings contributions, which are a transfer to the employee's own accounts, not a cost. Employer cost is not shown because the example is constructed to isolate the depuration ordering rule.
7780	The Colombian employer cost factor is not monotonic in salary. It rises at the low end because the contribution-base floor bites on part-time and low-paid work; it falls at the top end because of the all-inclusive salary's 70% rule; and it spikes at exactly 10 minimum wages where the exemption is lost. The cliff is quantified in Module 5 and demonstrated by the bonus example in Module 9.2.
7781	Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 127, 128, 129, 132, 149, 150, 151, 192, 230, 249, 306 (art. 150 as substituted by Law 1911 of 2018 art. 22); Law 100 of 1993 arts. 18, 20, 204; Law 21 of 1982 art. 17; Law 89 of 1988 art. 1; Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30; Law 1527 of 2012; Tax Code (Decree-Law 624 of 1989) arts. 55, 56, 114-1, 126-1, 126-4, 206 num. 10, 336 num. 3, 383, 385, 387, 388; Decree 1072 of 2015 arts. 2.2.4.3.3 and 2.2.4.3.5; Decree 1772 of 1994 art. 13; Decree 780 of 2016 art. 3.2.1.5 (substituted by Decree 1990 of 2016 art. 1); Decree 1469 of 2025; Decree 1470 of 2025; Resolution DIAN 000238 of 2025.
7782	
7783	Module 13: Common Business Practices & Non-Statutory Rules
7784	Purpose: To capture prevalent, non-statutory conventions.
7785	Status of this module. Everything below is market convention, not statute, except where a statutory constraint is expressly identified. These are the practices a Colombian payroll implementation will encounter and must be able to configure.
7786	13.1 Non-Statutory Conventions
7787	Describe the common practice for off-cycle or ad-hoc payments.
7788	Off-cycle payments are common in Colombia and are constrained by one structural fact: the electronic payroll document and the social-security contribution return are both monthly. An off-cycle payment therefore never creates a separate filing — it is aggregated into the same monthly artefacts.
7789	Practice
7790	Prevalence
7791	Handling
7792	The semi-monthly advance
7793	Dominant. The 15th is paid as a fixed advance of half the basic salary, with no withholding tax and no social-security deduction; the month-end payment settles everything
7794	Not truly off-cycle — it is the standard cycle. Both payments are aggregated into one monthly contribution base, one monthly withholding calculation and one monthly XML
7795	Termination settlement paid off-cycle
7796	Very common — settlements are paid within days of termination rather than waiting for the cycle
7797	Aggregated into the termination month's base for both tax and social security
7798	Off-cycle bonus
7799	Common — annual and performance bonuses are often paid on their own date
7800	Aggregated into the month. Tax effect = table(new month total) − already withheld
7801	Payroll error correction
7802	Common
7803	Where a payment was short, pay the shortfall off-cycle. Do not defer it — Labour Code art. 65 exposes the employer to one day's salary per day of delay at termination
7804	Salary advance (anticipo de salario)
7805	Common, typically 30% to 50% of the month's salary, recovered from the next payment
7806	Requires a written agreement stating the instalment and the amortisation period (Labour Code art. 151). Not a loan — an advance on salary already accruing
7807	Emergency or hardship payment
7808	Occasional
7809	Treated as an advance, or as an occasional gratuity (non-salary under art. 128)
7810	Off-cycle statutory benefit payment
7811	Occasional — the service bonus and severance-savings settlement are frequently paid on their own dates
7812	Statutory dates: 30 June and 20 December for the service bonus; January for the interest; 14 February for the severance-savings deposit
7813	
7814	Rules that govern any off-cycle payment.
7815	Aggregate into the month, do not create a second monthly artefact. One contribution return record,
7816	one XML document, one Form 350 line per employee per month. Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 art. 1.5.3.2.2 and DIAN Concepto 013840 (int. 1568) of 1 October 2025 are both explicit on monthly accumulation.
7817	Compute the incremental tax, not a fresh table read. Under Procedure 1 the correct withholding on
7818	the second payment is table(month total) − already withheld. A fresh read on the off-cycle payment alone applies the progressive bands twice from zero and systematically under-withholds.
7819	Re-test the monthly thresholds on the new total. The 10-minimum-wage exemption cut-off, the
7820	4-minimum-wage solidarity trigger, the 16-minimum-wage subsistence bands, the 25-minimum-wage ceiling and the 40% non-salary clamp are all monthly tests on the month's aggregate.
7821	Transmit the XML once, by the 10th of the following month, covering all payments of the month.
7822	Never use an off-cycle payment to defer a statutory date. The service-bonus and severance-savings
7823	dates are hard deadlines with day-rate penalties.
7824	A practical convention worth recording. Because the 15th advance carries no deductions, an employee who terminates between the 1st and the 15th will have received an advance against a month that will not be completed. The settlement must recover it, and the recovery is subject to the minimum-wage floor of Labour Code art. 149 num. 2 — so a large advance against a short final month can be irrecoverable through payroll and must be pursued separately.
7825	How are non-recoverable draws against future commissions typically handled?
7826	Commission arrangements are common in Colombian sales, insurance, real-estate and pharmaceutical roles. Commissions are salary by express statutory definition — Labour Code art. 127 lists "porcentajes sobre ventas y comisiones" — which shapes everything about how draws are handled.
7827	Practice
7828	Description
7829	Payroll treatment
7830	Guaranteed minimum plus commission
7831	The dominant Colombian structure. A fixed basic salary at or above the minimum wage, plus commission earned on top. There is no draw and no recovery
7832	Basic salary and commission are both salary. Both enter the contribution base, the payroll-levy base and the statutory-benefit base. Commission also enters the vacation base (Labour Code art. 192 excludes only overtime and rest-day work)
7833	Recoverable draw (anticipo de comisiones)
7834	A monthly advance against commissions not yet earned, recovered from later commission
7835	Structured as a salary advance under Labour Code art. 151, requiring a written agreement stating the instalment and the amortisation period. Recovery is subject to the minimum-wage floor of art. 149 num. 2
7836	Non-recoverable draw
7837	A guaranteed monthly amount that is not recovered if commissions fall short
7838	This is simply salary. It is not a loan, it is not recoverable, and it is fully contributory and fully taxable in the month it is paid. Colombia has no concept of a non-recoverable draw sitting outside salary
7839	Commission-only remuneration
7840	Legally permitted — Labour Code art. 132 num. 1 allows remuneration by unit of time, work, piece or task
7841	Constrained by the minimum wage. Where commissions in a month fall below the minimum wage, the employer must top up to the minimum wage. The top-up is salary
7842	
7843	Why Colombia rarely uses the classic non-recoverable draw. Four statutory constraints make it unattractive:
7844	The minimum-wage guarantee. The employer must in any event top up to the minimum wage, so a
7845	guaranteed floor already exists by operation of law.
7846	The contribution-base floor. Even a nil-commission month contributes on 1 minimum wage, so
7847	there is no contribution saving in a low month.
7848	Article 149 num. 2. Recovery of a draw may not take pay below the minimum wage without a court
7849	order, even with written authorisation — so recovery is often impossible in exactly the months where it is needed.
7850	Variable-pay averaging. Commissions enter the statutory-benefit bases on averaged figures — the
7851	service bonus on the semester average, severance savings on the average of the last year where the salary varied in the last 3 months (art. 253), vacation on the average of the preceding year (art. 192 num. 2). A draw structure does not reduce those averages.
7852	Handling the recovery of a draw where the employee terminates. The outstanding balance is set off against the termination settlement, but only within the art. 149 limits and only on the strength of the written agreement. Any residue is an ordinary civil debt, pursued outside payroll. Do not withhold the statutory benefits to force recovery — Labour Code art. 65's late-payment penalty of one day's salary per day of delay will exceed the draw within weeks.
7853	Configuration. Carry three distinct wage types: commission earned (salary, contributory, in the vacation base), commission advance (a recoverable payment governed by art. 151, with a balance), and commission guarantee top-up (salary). Maintain the rolling averages the statutory-benefit bases require.
7854	Are there common non-statutory leave types? How are they managed and reported?
7855	Yes — Colombia has an active market in extra-statutory leave, driven partly by collective agreements and partly by employer branding. None of it is regulated, and none of it is reported to any authority.
7856	Non-statutory leave
7857	Prevalence
7858	Typical terms
7859	Payroll treatment
7860	Additional vacation days
7861	Common in professional and multinational employers
7862	5 to 15 additional days on top of the statutory 15 working days
7863	Paid at ordinary salary. If genuinely additional paid rest, it enters the contribution base like statutory vacation. Cash compensation of the additional days is not restricted by the art. 189 half-limit, which applies to the statutory entitlement
7864	Birthday day off
7865	Very common
7866	One paid day per year
7867	Paid leave by employer decision; salary continues; contributions continue
7868	Christmas–New Year shutdown
7869	Common in manufacturing
7870	Collective shutdown, often charged against statutory vacation
7871	Where charged against vacation, treated as vacation. Where additional, paid leave
7872	Extended bereavement leave
7873	Common
7874	Beyond the statutory 5 working days, or extending to relatives outside the statutory degrees
7875	Paid leave; salary continues
7876	Extended paternity leave
7877	Increasingly common
7878	Beyond the statutory 2 weeks, employer-funded
7879	Employer-paid, not recoverable from the health insurer. Fully contributory and taxable
7880	Marriage leave
7881	Common
7882	3 to 5 paid days
7883	Paid leave
7884	Moving-house leave
7885	Occasional
7886	1 to 2 paid days
7887	Paid leave
7888	Study or examination leave
7889	Common in employers with education programmes
7890	Paid or unpaid
7891	As agreed
7892	Volunteering day
7893	Growing
7894	1 to 2 paid days per year
7895	Paid leave
7896	Sabbatical
7897	Rare, senior staff
7898	1 to 6 months, usually unpaid
7899	Unpaid leave — suspension mechanics apply: unpaid-leave change event, levies to 0%, accruals suspended (Module 8)
7900	"Flexible Friday" / short summer hours
7901	Common
7902	Early finish, no pay reduction
7903	A working-time arrangement, not leave. Must stay within the maximum-hours rules
7904	Additional sick days without a certificate
7905	Common
7906	2 to 3 days per year at full pay
7907	Employer-paid; not recoverable from the health insurer, which pays only on a certified incapacity from day 3
7908	
7909	Management and reporting.
7910	Aspect
7911	Treatment
7912	Legal basis
7913	Contract, internal work rules or collective agreement. Once granted habitually, an extra-statutory benefit may become a binding condition of employment through the principle of acquired rights, so it should be documented as revocable where the employer wants flexibility
7914	Contribution base
7915	Paid extra-statutory leave is paid rest and enters the contribution base and the payroll-levy base, exactly as statutory vacation does
7916	Statutory-benefit base
7917	Enters the service-bonus, severance-savings and interest bases as ordinary salary
7918	Tax
7919	Ordinary taxable employment income
7920	Statutory reporting
7921	None. No authority receives extra-statutory leave data. It appears in the electronic payroll XML only as ordinary remunerated leave or as part of the ordinary salary line
7922	Vacation register
7923	Labour Code art. 187 num. 3 requires a register of statutory vacation. Extra-statutory days should be tracked separately, so that the statutory 15 working days can be evidenced and the art. 190 accumulation limits applied to the right balance
7924	Termination
7925	Extra-statutory leave is not paid out on termination unless the contract or agreement says so. Only the statutory vacation entitlement must be settled in cash
7926	
7927	The configuration rule that matters. Keep two separate balances — statutory vacation and extra-statutory leave. They have different accrual rules, different accumulation limits, different cash compensation limits and, critically, different termination payout obligations. Merging them into one balance creates an unfunded termination liability on the extra-statutory portion and makes the statutory 15-day entitlement impossible to evidence.
7928	Other prevalent non-statutory practices worth recording
7929	Practice
7930	Prevalence
7931	Notes
7932	Non-salary pacts on a substantial share of remuneration
7933	Very widespread. The standard Colombian remuneration-structuring device
7934	Reduces the contribution base and the statutory-benefit base, up to the 40% clamp of Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30. No tax effect whatsoever. Employers commonly structure to just under 40%
7935	All-inclusive salary for senior staff
7936	Standard above 13 minimum wages
7937	Contribution and payroll-levy bases at 70%; three of the four statutory benefits absorbed; vacation is not
7938	Employer-paid prepaid health cover
7939	Very common for professional staff
7940	Structured as non-salary; the employee may deduct up to 16 tax units per month against the withholding base
7941	Meal vouchers and meal cards
7942	Common
7943	Tax Code art. 387-1: not income up to 41 tax units per month where salary ≤ 310 tax units. Must be paid to a third party — cash for food is salary
7944	Employer-funded voluntary pension contributions
7945	Common for senior staff
7946	Exempt income within the shared 30% / 3,800-unit cap; deductible for the employer up to 3,800 units per employee
7947	Extra-statutory Christmas bonus (prima extralegal de navidad)
7948	Common
7949	Set off against the statutory service bonus under Labour Code art. 308; the employer pays only the difference where the statutory bonus is larger
7950	Extra-statutory vacation bonus (prima de vacaciones)
7951	Common
7952	Usually structured as non-salary under a written art. 128 pact, so it stays out of the IBC up to the limit of Ley 1393 de 2010 art. 30: non-salary payments above 40% of the month's total remuneration enter the IBC at the ordinary flat rates. There is no 95-tax-unit or other bonus-specific threshold. If taxable, it is simply added to the month's other labour income for the ordinary art. 383/388 depuration. 95 tax units is the upper bound of the 0% first bracket of the general art. 383 withholding table, which applies to all labour income; it is not a bonus-specific rule and has no bearing on social security.
7953	Payroll lending (libranza)
7954	Extremely widespread — a mainstream consumer-credit channel in Colombia
7955	Law 1527 of 2012. The employer must deduct and remit; the aggregate must leave at least 50% of net salary. Employers routinely manage several concurrent lenders per employee
7956	Employee savings funds (fondos de empleados)
7957	Very common in larger employers
7958	Employee contributions deducted under Labour Code art. 150 without an individual order; employer contributions usually structured as non-salary
7959	Monthly advance deposit of severance savings
7960	New and growing
7961	Law 2466 of 2025 art. 64 permits monthly deposit of 8.33% to the fund, and written agreement to pay the 1% interest monthly. Smooths the February and January cash-flow spikes
7962	Advancing health-insurer benefits through payroll
7963	Universal practice
7964	The employer advances sickness, maternity and paternity benefits and recovers from the insurer, because the institution pays in arrears. The engine must model a benefit line that is paid to the employee but receivable from the institution
7965	Paying the 100% rest-day premium early
7966	Occasional
7967	Labour Code art. 179 transitional paragraph expressly permits an employer to adopt the 100% premium ahead of the 2027 date. Where it does, DIAN Concepto 013840 of 2025 confirms the differentiated percentages can coexist within a month without obstructing the electronic payroll filing
7968	Thirteenth and fourteenth salaries
7969	Not a Colombian practice
7970	Colombia's 13th-month equivalent is the statutory service bonus, paid in two halves. There is no 14th salary
7971	Tax equalisation for expatriates
7972	Common in multinationals
7973	Purely contractual; no statutory recognition. The grossed-up amount is itself taxable, so the calculation is iterative
7974	
7975	Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 65, 127, 128, 132, 149, 150, 151, 179, 187 num. 3, 189, 190, 192, 253, 306, 307, 308; Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30; Law 1527 of 2012; Law 2466 of 2025 arts. 14 and 64; Tax Code arts. 126-1, 206 num. 10, 387, 387-1, 388; Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 art. 1.5.3.2.2; DIAN Concepto 013840 int. 1568 of 1 October 2025.
